**Summary.** This work establishes that the speed at which entanglement can be generated in quantum networks is limited by two independent factors: the fixed entangling capacity of the physical interface and the ability of the internal network architecture to continuously supply fresh quantum resources. By analyzing fermionic Gaussian dynamics and complex graph structures, the authors provide sharp bounds and demonstrate that the architecture dictates the sustainability of entanglement generation over time.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, resource-theoretic understanding of speed limits in quantum information processing, distinguishing between local coupling strength and global connectivity/routing capability.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the fundamental speed limit for generating entanglement in quantum networks when the physical interface is fixed, but the internal routing architecture is dynamical and constrained.

**Main result.** Entanglement generation speed is governed by two distinct resources: the fixed interface capacity (entangling flux) and the ability of the surrounding architecture to continually replenish fresh degrees of freedom.

**Method.** The authors derive theoretical bounds using fermionic Gaussian dynamics and employ advanced optimization techniques like Variational Entanglement-Enhancing-Field (VEEF) optimization across various network topologies.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum network analyzed using fermionic Gaussian dynamics, often modeled by Ising chains or tree-tree architectures. The dynamics are controlled by local rotations and a fixed cross-cut interaction defining the interface.

**Key observables.** Canonical entanglement angles ($	heta_k$), bipartite entanglement in ebits ($E_A$), and the entanglement-generation speed ($\sum |\dot{	heta}_k|$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interface Capacity ($\Lambda_{\partial}$), Architectural Replenishment efficiency, and the network topology (e.g., open vs. periodic Ising chains).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to perform arbitrarily fast on-site rotations and focuses on the distinction between fixed interface flux and architectural replenishment capability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the saturation mechanism in open Ising chains, showing Majorana-rematching trajectories, and map the minimum saturation depth ($L^*$) required for different network topologies.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the core resource constraints, derives the coefficient-sharp bound for Gaussian dynamics, proves saturation using Ising chains, and then extends the analysis to complex tree-tree architectures using high-resolution VEEF optimization to classify the limiting topological resource.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that entanglement-generation speed across a fixed network interface is governed by two distinct resources: the entangling capacity of the interface itself and the ability of the surrounding architecture to replenish it with fresh degrees of freedom. For fermionic Gaussian dynamics, we derive the coefficient-sharp bound $\sum_k|\dotθ_k|\leq\frac12\|K_{AB}\|_*$ on the collective speed of the canonical entanglement angles. Explicit Ising-chain rematching trajectories saturate this bound, thereby certifying exact minimum interaction times under the stated control model. Beyond the Gaussian setting, exhaustive optimization of the complete $N=8$ tree--tree family shows that, at fixed interface capacity, first-layer entanglement, connectedness, and edge budget, the saturation depth is exactly classified by rooted architecture. With higher-resolution $x$-only control, variational entanglement-enhancing-field (VEEF) optimization reaches the numerically resolved fast-$X$ optimum in a two-channel benchmark. Across all 21 symmetry-reduced rooted orbits, a pre-specified two-time VEEF growth diagnostic recovers the complete replenishment partition directly from optimized dynamics. Interface capacity therefore sets how much entangling flux is available, whereas architecture determines whether fresh degrees of freedom can continually replenish the interface and sustain repeated use of that capacity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenging inverse problem of quantum mechanics: determining the full Hamiltonian from its energy-dependent, model-space effective Hamiltonian. The authors successfully develop a systematic, constructive algebraic method to solve this, showing that the reconstruction can be expressed in compact, stable recursive formulae, thereby resolving prior skepticism about the problem's solvability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to open quantum systems and many-body dynamics because it provides a rigorous mathematical framework for extracting underlying physics (the full Hamiltonian) from simplified, measurable, or computationally tractable effective descriptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the inverse problem of quantum mechanics: reconstructing the full-space Hamiltonian $H$ from its energy-dependent, effective Hamiltonian $H_{	ext{eff}}(E)$ derived via model-space projection.

**Main result.** The authors provide a systematic, constructive method to solve this inverse problem, demonstrating that the reconstruction can be achieved via compact, recursive algebraic formulae, even for large model-space extensions ($K$).

**Method.** The reconstruction involves solving a coupled set of polynomial algebraic equations derived from matching the known $H_{	ext{eff}}(E)$ to the full Hamiltonian structure, utilizing techniques like Gröbner-basis elimination and recurrence relation matching.

**Model / system.** The system is a general quantum mechanical system governed by a Hamiltonian $H$. The method employs a model-space projector $P$ to derive an effective Hamiltonian $H_{	ext{eff}}(E)$ that depends on the energy $E$. The full Hamiltonian $H$ is assumed to have a specific, partially tridiagonal structure.

**Key observables.** The energy-dependent effective Hamiltonian $H_{	ext{eff}}(E)$ and the unknown matrix elements of the full Hamiltonian $H(N)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** M (model space dimension), K (difference in dimensions, $N-M$), and the energy $E$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the Hamiltonian $H$ can be effectively described by a finite-dimensional, partially tridiagonal structure, allowing the problem to be reduced to polynomial algebra.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference Table 7, which displays reconstructed matrix elements $a_2$ for various values of $K$ (up to $K=8$), illustrating the systematic nature of the reconstruction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the inverse problem using the Feshbach projection formalism, establishing the necessary algebraic equations, and then developing a constructive solution by showing how the complexity of the resulting polynomial equations stabilizes into a manageable recursive form for increasing $K$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Given a certain specific, $M$ by $M$ matrix form of the Feshbach's effective (i.e., energy-dependent) Hamiltonian $H^{(M)}_{e\!f\!f}(E)$, the inverse-problem reconstruction of the full-space, $N$ by $N$ matrix Hamiltonian $H^{(N)}$ is considered and reduced to the solution of a coupled set of polynomial algebraic equations. Using computer-assisted symbolic manipulations, an explicit algebraic reconstruction of $H^{(N)}$ is found feasible at not too large $K = N-M$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work adapts Variational Quantum Algorithms to solve complex nonlinear elasticity problems for hyperelastic materials. It tackles nonlinear constitutive laws by reformulating the energy functional into a quantum-compatible cost function. The key contribution is an iterative correction strategy that significantly boosts the accuracy of the quantum mechanical solution.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is in continuum mechanics, the core methodology—encoding a complex physical energy functional into a quantum cost function and using iterative quantum optimization—is highly relevant to simulating complex quantum many-body Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses extending Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQA) to solve nonlinear elasticity problems involving constitutive nonlinearities with rational powers of the stretch.

**Main result.** The proposed iterative correction strategy significantly improves the accuracy of the quantum solution for hyperelastic models like Ogden and Mooney-Rivlin compared to direct VQA or penalty methods.

**Method.** It reformulates the potential energy functional as a cost function minimized by a VQA, employing auxiliary variables and penalty constraints to handle complex nonlinear terms.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on hyperelasticity, specifically using one-dimensional incompressible Ogden and Mooney-Rivlin models governed by an elastostatic boundary-value problem.

**Key observables.** The cost function $C(	heta_0, 	heta)$ is minimized to find the equilibrium state, and the relative $L_2$ errors (%) compared to analytical solutions are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The penalty parameter $eta$, and the control parameters $(	heta_0, 	heta)$ defining the quantum state are important.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on approximating the strain-energy density $W$ using polynomial expansions and assumes that the cost function minimization yields the unique physical solution.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall iterative correction loop. Figure 4 compares the quantum solutions (analytical, penalty-based, and iteratively corrected) for various models, showing displacement fields and relative $L_2$ errors.

**Paper structure.** The paper outlines the VQA formulation for the potential energy, details the transformation of nonlinearities using auxiliary variables, presents the two-stage minimization process (initial VQA followed by iterative correction VQAs), and concludes with numerical demonstrations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper extends a recently proposed Variational Quantum Algorithm framework for nonlinear elasticity to a broader class of constitutive nonlinearities involving rational powers of the stretch. One-dimensional incompressible Ogden and Mooney-Rivlin models are employed as representative examples to demonstrate the proposed methodology. Nonlinear constitutive terms are transformed into forms compatible with the available quantum algorithmic primitives through the introduction of auxiliary variables and penalty constraints, yielding approximate solutions via a Variational Quantum Algorithm. An iterative correction strategy based on a sequence of Variational Quantum Algorithms is then introduced to improve solution accuracy. A Numerical example demonstrates the proposed approach.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep algebraic characterization of Tree Tensor Network (TTN) varieties, proving they are defined by minors of matrix flattenings and are equivalent to general Markov models. Furthermore, it develops a combinatorial method to compute the degree of these varieties, which is a crucial invariant for understanding the complexity of quantum states represented by tensor networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between tensor network representations (used extensively in simulating strongly correlated electron models) and algebraic geometry provides powerful, rigorous tools for analyzing the complexity and structure of quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the algebraic structure, specifically the prime ideal, of tree tensor network varieties ($	ext{TTN}_{T,V,r}$). It also focuses on computing the degree of these varieties for specific cases like order 3 tensor trains.

**Main result.** The prime ideal of the TTN variety is generated by the minors of matrix flattenings, and the variety is shown to be equivalent to a general Markov model on the associated spaced tree. A combinatorial method is provided to compute the degree for order 3 tensor trains.

**Method.** The authors employ algebraic geometry, viewing the variety as a projective algebraic variety defined by rank constraints on matrix flattenings. They use techniques involving initial ideals and non-intersecting path counting to determine the degree.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Tree Tensor Network (TTN), which is a mathematical tool used to represent high-order tensors. These networks are relevant in quantum many-body physics, particularly for describing 2D quantum systems, and are related to tensor train and subspace decompositions.

**Key observables.** The prime ideal generators (minors of matrix flattenings) and the degree of the variety are key algebraic observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the rooted tree $T$, the attached vector spaces $V$, and the admissible rank sequence $r = (r_v)$ are the critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on relating the TTN variety to general Markov models and utilizing results from the theory of determinantal and initial ideals for degree computation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of TTNs (e.g., binary TTN of order 4) and show how the TTN structure realizes both TT and Subspace decompositions for order 3.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the algebraic characterization of the TTN variety using minors (Theorem 1.1). It then proves the equivalence between TTN, TT, and Subspace varieties (Proposition 2.5). Finally, it focuses on computing the degree of the TT variety using combinatorial methods based on initial ideals (Proposition 6.7).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that tree tensor network varieties, including tensor train varieties, are general Markov models associated to spaced trees. This allows us to prove that the prime ideals of these varieties are generated by minors of matrix flattenings. In the case of tensor train varieties, we discuss whether these minors form a Gröbner basis and provide a combinatorial method to compute the degree for order $3$ tensor trains.

</details>

**Summary.** This technical proposal outlines the AICE facility, a large-scale atom interferometer planned for CERN to search for ultralight dark matter and test fundamental physics principles like the Equivalence Principle. By utilizing long baselines and various atomic species, it aims to achieve unprecedented precision in measuring fundamental constants and gravitational effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a major application of advanced cold atom techniques (atom interferometry) to fundamental physics, pushing the scale of terrestrial measurements significantly beyond current limits. It requires expertise in vacuum technology, laser cooling, and quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is probing for bosonic ultralight dark matter (ULDM) in a mass range inaccessible to other experiments, with a secondary goal of exploring gravitational waves (GWs) in the $\sim 0.03-3$ Hz range.

**Main result.** The proposal details the technical feasibility of the AICE facility, confirming that the PX46 site is mature and ready for installation to conduct these precision measurements.

**Method.** The experiment uses long-baseline atom interferometry, employing ultracold atoms in a gradiometer geometry to measure phase shifts sensitive to external fields and fundamental constants.

**Model / system.** The platform is the Atom Interferometer CERN Experiment (AICE), a $\mathcal{O}(100)$ m vertical interferometer utilizing ultracold $^{87}	ext{Sr}$ (with planned upgrades to $^{88}	ext{Sr}$ and $^{171}	ext{Yb}$) atoms.

**Key observables.** Dark matter coupling strengths, gravitational wave signals, violation of the Equivalence Principle (EP), and the fine-structure constant ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Baseline length of $\sim 100$ m; GW frequency range of $\sim 0.03-3$ Hz; target sensitivity for $\alpha$ and EP violation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proposal assumes the suitability and maturity of the PX46 site for installation without impacting LHC operations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a general schematic view of AICE in the PX46 shaft, confirming compatibility with LHC component transport.

**Paper structure.** The proposal is structured to cover the motivation, the experimental concept (including gradiometer and multi-species operation), the detailed physics case (ULDM, $\alpha$, GWs), the technical detector systems, and the necessary site integration/civil engineering plans.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the technical proposal for the Atom Interferometer CERN Experiment (AICE), a $\mathcal{O}(100)$ m vertical atom interferometer to be installed against the wall of the PX46 access shaft to the LHC. AICE is conceived as a versatile and flexible long-baseline atom-interferometry facility whose primary scientific goal is probing for bosonic ultralight dark matter (ULDM) in a mass range inaccessible to other experiments, with a secondary goal of pioneering the exploration of gravitational waves (GWs) with frequencies in the range ${\sim}$0.03-3 Hz as a pathfinder for future longer-baseline detectors. The initial configuration employs ultracold $^{87}$Sr atoms in a single-photon 698-nm interferometer with three shaft-based atom sources in a multi-source gradiometer geometry, supported by one surface reference source for laser stabilisation and diagnostics, to target scalar ULDM. Operation with $^{88}$Sr will give sensitivity to axion-like particles (ALPs), vector ULDM with $B-L$ couplings and violation of the principle of equivalence, while a $^{171}$Yb upgrade will improve the sensitivity to $B-L$ couplings and equivalence violations. Probing the Einstein equivalence principle (EP) and measuring $α$ will proceed in parallel with the ULDM searches. A conceptual feasibility study and a detailed technical implementation study have established that PX46 is a uniquely mature and implementation-ready site, with no technical showstoppers. Completing site preparation works during LS3 would enable the subsequent installation and operation of AICE without impacting HL-LHC operations. The detector design builds on the VLBAI and MAGIS experiments and the AION-10 Technical Design Report, scaling the strontium gradiometer architecture to the $\sim$100 m baseline. AICE is endorsed by the TVLBAI Proto-Collaboration, comprising 57 institutions in 22 countries.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes the electronic and excitonic properties of AgCrP2S6, a magnetic semiconductor. By combining structural measurements with advanced many-body calculations, the authors determine that the low-energy optical excitation is a localized Frenkel exciton. This highlights the delicate balance between covalent bonding and magnetic interactions in determining optical response in correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of exciton localization and the explicit calculation of exciton-phonon coupling channels provide a rich model for understanding how lattice vibrations modulate electronic excitations in strongly correlated magnetic insulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the competition between covalency, magnetic localization, and structural symmetry in determining the nature of the lowest optical exciton in the antiferromagnetic semiconductor AgCrP2S6.

**Main result.** The lowest exciton is found to be predominantly a localized Frenkel exciton, despite increased covalency, due to the combined effects of antiferromagnetism and Ag-site disorder.

**Method.** The research combines single-crystal X-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy, lattice-dynamical calculations, and advanced many-body theory (vertex corrected Feynman diagrammatic approaches).

**Model / system.** The system is the layered thiophosphate semiconductor AgCrP2S6, which features an antiferromagnetic Cr sublattice and nonmagnetic Ag sites, allowing for the study of competing electronic interactions.

**Key observables.** Lowest exciton energy (~1.4 eV), exciton character (Frenkel vs. Wannier-Mott), exciton-phonon coupling channels, and quasiparticle gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (100-300 K), exciton binding energy (~770 meV), and the relative strength of p-d hybridization versus intersite coherence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the material remains dynamically stable in its monoclinic phase and that the low-energy optical response is dominated by $q=0$ excitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, band structure along high-symmetry paths, and detailed analysis of the exciton character, real-space hole density, and phonon dispersion/Raman spectra.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from structural characterization (XRD, lattice dynamics) to electronic structure (quasiparticle gap) and finally to the detailed excitonic and exciton-phonon coupling analysis using advanced many-body techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

AgCrP2S6 combines a low-symmetry thiophosphate framework with an antiferromagnetic Cr sublattice and nonmagnetic Ag sites, providing a setting in which covalency and magnetic localization compete in the low-energy optical response. We combine single-crystal x-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy, lattice-dynamical calculations, and self-consistent vertex corrected Feynman diagrammatic many-body approaches to determine the structural, electronic, and excitonic properties of bulk AgCrP2S6. The material remains monoclinic between 100 and 300 K, with additional Ag-site disorder resolved at low temperature, and the optimized structure is dynamically stable. The many-body calculations yield a reduced quasiparticle gap relative to Cr trihalides, but the lowest excitons remain predominantly local: the calculated low-energy (~1.4 eV) excitation cluster is best described as a weakly bright, strongly anisotropic Frenkel exciton with dominant onsite d-d character and substantial ligand-assisted d-p admixture. Symmetry analysis in the C2h setting identifies this state as predominantly Bu and constrains its leading exciton-phonon coupling channels to Ag diagonal renormalization and Bg-mediated bright-dark mixing. These results place AgCrP2S6 in a localized excitonic regime in which the stronger p-d hybridization, relative to the more ionic Cr trihalides, narrows the quasiparticle gap, while the antiferromagnetic exchange and Ag-site dilution disfavor the intersite coherence associated with strongly delocalized low-energy excitons.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> Figure 5. RushHour overview. The compiler (§6) sched- ules against the LMU interface (§5). The LMU manages the dynamic lattice and realizes gates as RushHour ISA instruc- tions (§4).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces RushHour, a novel hardware-compiler co-design for performing lattice surgery in fault-tolerant quantum computing. It overcomes the rigidity of existing methods by enabling dynamic reconfiguration of the quantum lattice in real-time. This results in significantly improved resource efficiency, allowing complex quantum algorithms to run on much smaller and faster physical quantum chips.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconducting/trapped-ion architectures, the core concepts of dynamic resource management, scheduling, and hardware-software co-design are highly relevant to optimizing complex quantum control protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Achieving practical fault-tolerant quantum computing requires efficient lattice surgery (LS) to fit large algorithms onto resource-constrained quantum chips.

**Main result.** The proposed RushHour architecture significantly improves resource utilization, enabling benchmarks to run on much smaller chips and achieving median speedups of 2.0-7.2$	imes$ on space-constrained hardware.

**Method.** The authors propose a hardware-compiler co-design called RushHour, which formalizes dynamic lattice management via a new ISA and a Lattice Management Unit (LMU).

**Model / system.** The system models fault-tolerant quantum computation using the surface code, where logical operations are performed via dynamic lattice surgery (LS) on patches of physical qubits.

**Key observables.** Space-time trade-off, benchmark success rate on small chips, execution time (speedup factor), and resource footprint.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chip size constraints, logical error exposure, and the efficiency of dynamic reconfiguration (achieved in a single round).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that dynamic lattice management can be abstracted and scheduled efficiently by the compiler, allowing for non-blocking reconfigurations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between static vs. dynamic LS, the performance advantage of RushHour across different chip sizes, and the detailed structure of the dynamic lattice configuration ($\Sigma$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of static LS, proposes the dynamic LS model, details the RushHour ISA and LMU components, and concludes with extensive benchmarking comparing RushHour against multiple state-of-the-art compilers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Practical fault-tolerant quantum computing (FTQC) requires efficient lattice surgery (LS), so that large algorithms fit on resource-constrained quantum chips. Existing approaches, however, are rigid: qubits, routing space, and resource states are allocated ahead of execution, which prevents running on small chips, leaves statically scheduled executions with large time overheads, and fixes each design at a single area of the space-time trade-off.   We present dynamic LS, which enables efficient reconfiguration of the ancilla space, just-in-time allocation of resource states, and dynamic rotations of logical qubits, thereby spanning the entire space-time trade-off with a single, unified approach. We realize dynamic LS with RushHour through a hardware-compiler co-design: the RushHour ISA formalizes and programs our dynamic lattice model, the Lattice Management Unit abstracts dynamic lattice management and performs efficient lattice reconfiguration, and the RushHour Compiler compiles logical circuits for physical chips into optimized ISA programs while pipelining instructions.   We evaluate RushHour against six state-of-the-art compilers and two resource models. On the smallest chips, 86% of benchmarks run only with RushHour, while existing approaches require 1.2-3.5$\times$ larger chips. On space-constrained early-FTQC chips, RushHour runs a median 2.0-7.2$\times$ faster than the best feasible alternative, while achieving results comparable to the state of the art on very large chips. RushHour's constructive results run 4.8$\times$ from an idealized-machine resource limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that for ML models predicting material properties, the inclusion of parity labels in the model's features is the single design element that dictates whether the model respects exact physical symmetries. Models lacking this label frequently predict physically impossible, non-zero values for properties that must vanish due to crystal symmetry.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter ML, the rigorous mathematical framework for enforcing exact physical laws (like zero tensors) through structural design choices is highly relevant to building reliable quantum simulation models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses whether machine learning models designed for materials can predict physically impossible properties due to insufficient incorporation of exact symmetry constraints.

**Main result.** The ability to enforce exact symmetry constraints (like zero for centrosymmetric materials) is determined solely by a single design choice: whether the model's features are explicitly labeled with parity information.

**Method.** The authors derive the 'parity gap' from group theory to quantify the difference between approximate (rotational) and exact symmetry constraints, testing this using ML models trained on materials data.

**Model / system.** The system is crystalline materials, and the models are machine-learned surrogates for first-principles calculations, specifically tested on properties like the piezoelectric tensor.

**Key observables.** The predicted magnitude of the piezoelectric tensor, and the 'false-flag fraction' (percentage of predictions exceeding a physical threshold when the true value is zero).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parity label (a binary design choice), the symmetry group ($	ext{E}(3)$ vs $	ext{SO}(3)$), and the physical threshold ($	au = 0.01 	ext{ C m}^{-2}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of permutation and translation invariance, and crucially, that the parity label is an 'upstream' design choice.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the predictions of parity-labelled ($	ext{O}(3)$) vs. parity-blind ($	ext{SO}(3)$) models for the piezoelectric tensor, showing a separation of six orders of magnitude, with the $	ext{SO}(3)$ model frequently predicting forbidden, non-zero responses.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical distinction between approximate and exact symmetry constraints, derives the parity gap criterion, and then empirically demonstrates this gap by comparing the performance of ML architectures that either incorporate or ignore parity labels when predicting physical tensors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Machine-learned models are replacing first-principles calculations across materials discovery, and physical symmetry is the central guarantee built into them. The debate over how much symmetry to hard-wire rather than learn has run on rotations, where a symmetry error is an approximation error. Some constraints are exact: symmetry forces certain property tensors to exactly zero, so a nonzero prediction is physically impossible rather than inaccurate. Here we show that whether a model can make such predictions is decided before training by one rarely reported design bit, whether its features carry parity labels, and derive a criterion, the parity gap, that computes from group theory alone which properties and crystals are exposed. Across matched architecture pairs differing only in that bit, evaluated on two thousand centrosymmetric crystals whose piezoelectric tensor must vanish, parity-labelled arms sit at the floating-point floor while rotation-only arms predict forbidden responses on 90-96% of crystals, six orders of magnitude apart, at no accuracy cost. Training on explicit zeros does not recover exactness, and a head on a frozen universal potential inherits its backbone's symmetry group. One reflection at random initialization verifies the label in seconds.

</details>

### [Asymptotic Entanglement Hiding under Stabilizer Restrictions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18440v1)

**Authors:** Jicun Li, Wei Xie, Jun Wu, Honglin Chen, Xiang-Yang Li  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18440v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.18440_figures/2608.18440_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Mixed-stabilizer visibility gap. For ρ(s) = (1 −s)σ∗+ sρ∗, the solid curve gives the exact unre- stricted value EALL(ρ(s)) = D(ρ(s)∥σ∗). The dashed curve is DKL(Wρ(s)∥Wσ∗), the Wigner–KL upper bound on ESTAB(ρ(s)). Since this certified upper bound is strictly smaller than EALL(ρ(s)) for every s &gt; 0, stabilizer measure- ments are strictly weaker than unrestricted measurements for this mixed-stabilizer family.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a fundamental separation between entanglement and 'magic' by quantifying how much entanglement remains visible when measurements are restricted to stabilizer operations. Through explicit constructions, the authors show that entanglement can be asymptotically hidden, meaning the distillable entanglement can be large while the stabilizer-visible entanglement vanishes. This reveals intrinsic limitations on entanglement extraction using only stabilizer operations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantitative measure of how much quantum correlation is 'hidden' from computation protocols relying only on stabilizer measurements, which is crucial for understanding the limits of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental operational difference between entanglement visible under general measurements (LOCC) versus entanglement visible only through stabilizer measurements.

**Main result.** They demonstrate 'magic-free asymptotic entanglement hiding,' showing that highly entangled states can possess arbitrarily large LOCC-distillable entanglement while having vanishing entanglement visible under stabilizer restrictions.

**Method.** The analysis involves constructing explicit convex mixtures of stabilizer states and applying bounds derived from the relative entropy and Wigner function analysis to quantify the gap between different entanglement measures.

**Model / system.** The study uses bipartite quantum states, specifically focusing on $N$ qutrits per party, Haar-random pure states, and Werner states, all analyzed within the framework of stabilizer operations.

**Key observables.** Stabilizer-visible entanglement ($	ext{ESTAB}$), Unrestricted visible entanglement ($	ext{EALL}$), and LOCC-distillable entanglement ($ED_{LOCC}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qutrits per party ($N$), the local dimension ($p$, an odd prime), and the state parameter ($t$ for Werner states).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is the restriction of measurements to the set of stabilizer operations, which defines the 'magic' resource theory.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entanglement hiding mechanism by comparing the unrestricted visible entanglement (solid curve) to the stabilizer-restricted bound (dashed curve) for an interpolated state.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the measures, then proving the gap for specific states (Werner states, mixed stabilizer states), and finally establishing the asymptotic scaling laws for general mixed stabilizer states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement is central to quantum information processing, while stabilizer operations underpin fault-tolerant quantum computation. We ask how much entanglement remains visible or distillable under stabilizer restrictions. We quantify stabilizer-visible entanglement by restricting the measured relative entropy of entanglement to stabilizer measurements, thereby obtaining converse bounds on entanglement distillation under stabilizer operations. We demonstrate magic-free asymptotic entanglement hiding: we construct explicit convex mixtures of pure stabilizer states on $N$ qutrits per party whose unrestricted visible entanglement and LOCC-distillable entanglement both grow as $Ω(N/\log N)$, while their stabilizer-visible and stabilizer-distillable entanglement vanish as $N\to\infty$. Thus, an unbounded amount of LOCC-distillable entanglement carried by stabilizer states can become asymptotically invisible and undistillable under stabilizer restrictions. We further prove that stabilizer-visible entanglement is $O(1)$ with high probability for Haar-random pure states despite extensive unrestricted visibility, and vanishes uniformly over entangled Werner states as the local dimension grows through odd primes. These results reveal a fundamental separation between entanglement and magic as resources, exposing intrinsic limits on entanglement extraction using stabilizer operations.

</details>

### [Generalizing Pauli Checks for Qudit-based Quantum Error Detection and Mitigation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18332v1)

**Authors:** Noble Agyeman-Bobie, Quinn Langfitt, Salahedeen Issa, Nikos Hardavellas, Kaitlin N. Smith  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18332v1>

**Summary.** This paper extends Pauli Check Sandwiching (PCS) from qubits to qudits of arbitrary dimension $d$ for quantum error detection and mitigation. It develops generalized checks using the Heisenberg-Weyl set, proving that these checks can deterministically identify arbitrary qudit errors. The results validate the technique numerically, showing high fidelity preservation even under realistic error rates.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly advances the theoretical toolkit for fault-tolerant quantum computation by extending established error correction paradigms to higher-dimensional quantum systems (qudits), which are relevant for many physical implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes the Pauli Check Sandwiching (PCS) technique, traditionally used for qubit error detection and mitigation, to arbitrary qudit dimensions.

**Main result.** The generalized PCS protocol successfully detects arbitrary Heisenberg-Weyl errors in qudit systems, achieving unit fidelity in the noiseless check limit.

**Method.** It introduces generalized left and right Pauli checks using the Heisenberg-Weyl operator set, relying on commutation relations and Fourier orthogonality for error syndrome extraction.

**Model / system.** The model involves qudit systems of arbitrary dimension $d$, where error detection is performed by sandwiching the computation between controlled Pauli checks.

**Key observables.** Error-mitigated fidelity (validated above 97.5% for $d=2$ to $d=9$), and the deterministic mapping of an error to a unique ancilla readout.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the qudit space, and the depolarizing error rate $p$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the error channel can be decomposed into the Pauli (or Heisenberg-Weyl) basis, and that the checks themselves are noiseless.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase kickback in qubits and the general PCS circuit layout for both qubit and qudit systems, showing the structure of the error checks.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the generalization of PCS to qudits using the Heisenberg-Weyl set, mathematically proves the detection capability via commutation relations, and concludes with numerical validation on specific circuits (Mod-2 and GHZ-2) across various dimensions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pauli Check Sandwiching (PCS) is a quantum error detection (QED) technique that protects a quantum circuit by utilizing a pair of controlled Pauli operators, or checks, and detecting errors that anti-commute with the checks. Further, PCS can be used for quantum error mitigation (QEM) via post-selection based on the Pauli check syndrome values. Currently, PCS is leveraged in the qubit space. In this paper, we introduce a generalized approach for applying PCS-based QED and QEM to quantum information of arbitrary dimension in the Hilbert space. Each pair of these extended checks consists of a sequence of gates in the Heisenberg-Weyl operator set that extend Pauli operators into the qudit space. These qudit checks use at least one ancilla qudit to detect qudit errors that do not commute with the unitary selected for the check. We show that our proposed methods for qudit QED can detect errors of arbitrary dimensions. More specifically, we prove that an arbitrary Heisenberg-Weyl error maps deterministically to a unique ancilla readout, and further, post-selecting on the $|0 \rangle$ readout guarantees unit fidelity in the noiseless check limit. We validate these findings numerically across dimensions $d=2$ through $d=9$, achieving error-mitigated fidelities above $97.5\% $ under realistic depolarizing error rates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper bridges the gap between the Random Cluster Model and the Renormalization Group theory to provide rigorous mathematical proofs for the RG flow of the Ising model away from its critical point. By mapping the RG flow onto a simple 1D dynamical system based on cluster connectivity, the authors prove convergence to high and low-temperature fixed points. This work advances the mathematical understanding of critical phenomena in statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical techniques involving dynamical systems and convergence proofs are highly relevant to understanding the dynamics of quantum many-body systems and quantum phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a rigorous mathematical foundation for the Renormalization Group (RG) flow, specifically by connecting RG transformations to geometrical representations like the random cluster model.

**Main result.** The paper rigorously proves the convergence of the RG flow for the nearest-neighbor Ising model away from criticality to the zero- and infinite-temperature fixed points for a large family of transformations.

**Method.** The authors relate the scaling limit of the RG flow to a simple one-dimensional dynamical system governed by the cluster connectivity derived from the random cluster representation.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Ising model on the 2D square lattice ($\mathbb{Z}^2$), analyzed using the Random Cluster Model (RCM) framework. The analysis extends to higher-state Potts models.

**Key observables.** Convergence of the RG flow to trivial fixed points, correlation functions, and susceptibility ($\chi_p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), the RCM parameter $p$, and the RG transformation parameters ($\alpha, eta, \gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach is weakened by the need for negative cluster-weights, and the results are explicitly established for the nearest-neighbor Ising model, with generalizations being an open question.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dynamics of the RG flow parameter $f$ for different parameter regimes and show possible block configurations for coarse-graining in the 3-states Potts model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the need for rigorous RG foundations, detailing the RCM representation, analyzing the $2 	imes 2 	o 1$ RG transformation on the square lattice, and concluding with discussions on generalizations to higher dimensions and Potts models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many rigorous results in the modern era of statistical mechanics have been obtained through geometrical representations. A much studied tool in this setting is the random cluster representation of lattice spin models. Another area of statistical mechanics that is of great interest but lacking rigorous results is the theory of the renormalization group. This paper investigates the idea to find a common ground between these two concepts in order to obtain rigorous results on the renormalization group flow. A need for negative cluster-weights weakens the success of this approach. Nevertheless, we managed to establish a relation between the scaling limit of the renormalization group flow of the nearest-neighbour Ising model away from criticality with a simple one-dimensional dynamical system that follows the cluster connectivity of the renormalization group transformation. This allows for a rigorous proof of the convergence of this flow to the zero- and infinite-temperature fixed point respectively for a large family of renormalization group transformations. Explicit results will be established on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ followed by a discussion of the available generalizations to higher dimensions.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper rigorously analyzes when the reduced dynamics of a quantum system coupled to an environment remains linear. It proves that linearity requires the total system-environment unitary evolution to be factorisable. Crucially, it demonstrates that any non-factorisable evolution necessarily leads to a deviation from simple linear or completely positive dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental mathematical constraints on the description of open quantum systems, showing that simple assumptions (like CP maps) fail when initial correlations are present and the total evolution is non-factorisable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which the reduced dynamics of a quantum system interacting with an environment remains linear, particularly when the total system-environment unitary evolution is non-factorisable.

**Main result.** The reduced dynamics is linear for arbitrary initial states if and only if the total unitary evolution $U$ is factorisable ($U=U_S \otimes U_E$). Furthermore, non-factorisable evolution implies deviations from linearity or complete positivity.

**Method.** The analysis uses the mathematical framework of reduced density operators ($	ext{Tr}_E \circ 	ext{Ad}_U(ho_{SE})$) and compares the dynamics derived from different assumptions (e.g., CP maps vs. general linear maps) to deduce constraints on the unitary $U$.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a quantum system $S$ coupled to an environment $E$, evolving unitarily under $U$. The dynamics are studied by analyzing the reduced density matrix $ho_S = 	ext{Tr}_E(ho_{SE})$.

**Key observables.** Reduced dynamics ($ho'_S$), deviation from complete positivity (CP), and linearity of the reduced map.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the initial state set $\mathcal{S}$ relative to the full set $\mathcal{D}$, and the factorisability of the unitary $U$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often starts by considering the simplest approximation (CP map) and then generalizes to arbitrary initial states and unitaries to find where this approximation breaks down.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the necessary and sufficient condition for linear reduced dynamics (factorisability of $U$). It then uses this result to show that non-factorisable $U$ forces deviations from linearity or CP, culminating in proofs that demonstrate this deviation always occurs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the simplest approximation, the reduced dynamics of a quantum system $S$ interacting with its environment $E$ is considered to be given by a completely positive map. But, in general, this is not the case. In fact, the reduced dynamics of the system in not even linear, in general. Whether the reduced dynamics is linear or not is determined by two factors: the set of possible initial states of the system-environment $\mathcal{S}=\left\lbrace ρ_{SE} \right\rbrace $, and the joint system-environment unitary evolution $U$. When $U$ is factorisable as $U=U_S\otimes U_E$, then we can choose $\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{D}$, where $\mathcal{D}$ is the set of all system-environment density operators. In other words, when $U$ is factorisable, the reduced dynamics of the system $S$ is linear (in fact unitary) for arbitrary initial state of the system-environment $ρ_{SE}$. We show that this result cannot be generalized to any non-factorisable $U$: For any non-factorisable unitary evolution of the whole system-environment $U$, the set $\mathcal{S}$ must be chosen as a proper subset of $\mathcal{D}$ to achieve linear reduced dynamics. As a byproduct, considering a convex set of possible initial states of the system-environment $\mathcal{S}$ such that $\mathrm{Tr}_{E} \ \mathcal{S}=\mathrm{Tr}_{E} \ \mathcal{D}$, we show that when the reduced dynamics of the system, for one system-environment unitary evolution $U_1$, is positive, but not completely positive, this implies that reduced dynamics is not linear for another $U_2$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using quantum dots as highly tunable targets in a dark matter detector array. By calculating how DM scatters off electrons in QDs of varying sizes and surface chemistries, the authors show that the resulting pattern of energy deposition rates—a 'barcode'—can uniquely identify the underlying physics of the dark matter interaction.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core application is particle astrophysics, the underlying physics involves calculating electronic structure responses (form factors, energy thresholds) in nanoscale, confined systems, which touches upon condensed matter theory relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the search for dark matter (DM) interactions, specifically focusing on detecting low-mass DM (below a GeV) through inelastic energy deposits.

**Main result.** The relative rates of energy deposition across an array of quantum dot targets form a unique 'barcode' that can allow discrimination between different hypothesized DM interaction operators and mediator types.

**Method.** The authors perform ab initio calculations of DM scattering on electrons bound within quantum dots, modeling the system using effective operators and calculating material response functions.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a detector array using semiconductor quantum dots (e.g., Si QDs) as target subunits, read out by Skipper CCDs. The calculations treat the QDs as finite, non-periodic objects.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the relative scattering rates across different QD morphologies, which form the 'barcode'; also analyzed are the material response functions $W_1(q, E)$ and $W_4(q, E)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the QD diameter, surface ligand type, DM mass, interaction operator couplings, and the energy threshold set by the QD electronic gap ($\Delta E$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the non-relativistic limit and that the initial and final state momenta are zero due to the finite nature of the QD environment. The DM flux is modeled using a standard halo model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the detector concept (CAD rendering of QD/Skipper CCD setup) and plot the material response functions ($W_1$ and $W_4$) as functions of momentum transfer and energy, highlighting kinematically inaccessible regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system (QDs) and the detection concept (barcode) to detailing the theoretical framework (ab initio calculations of scattering amplitude using effective operators) and finally presenting the diagnostic power of the barcode strategy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum dots are tunable semiconductor nanocrystals that can be produced at industrial scales. We present the first ab initio calculation of the scattering of dark matter on electrons bound in quantum dots. The momentum-dependence of a quantum dot's electronic response depends on its morphology and on the dark matter mass, interaction operator, mediator coupling, and mediator mass. Therefore, the relative rates across an array of distinct quantum dot targets form a ``barcode'' that carries information about the nature of the dark matter interaction. We project the sensitivity of a detector concept in which a collection of independent target subunits, each loaded with silicon quantum dots of a particular morphology, are read out by Skipper CCDs. Given a future signal, this barcode could discriminate between interaction operators and mediator types. We quantify the discrimination power for a benchmark pair of models as a function of readout noise and exposure.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present TDSE-Z, a high-performance, open-source framework for simulating strong-field quantum dynamics in materials with position-dependent effective mass. It uses a weak-form Galerkin method on B-splines to solve the time-dependent Schrödinger equation. The code is validated by reproducing known physical results, such as WKB decay in quantum wells and HHG spectra, making it a powerful tool for quantum optics and semiconductor physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly robust and general computational tool for simulating light-matter interactions in solid-state and molecular systems, which is central to quantum optics and condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of simulating strong-field quantum dynamics in complex materials, particularly those with spatially varying effective mass at interfaces.

**Main result.** The developed framework, TDSE-Z, successfully implements the BenDaniel-Duke operator using a weak-form Galerkin approach, validating its accuracy across static eigensolvers (WKB comparison) and time propagation (HHG spectra).

**Method.** The core method involves a weak-form Galerkin discretization of the time-dependent Schrödinger equation using B-spline basis functions on geometry-adapted meshes.

**Model / system.** The system models atomic, molecular, and semiconductor effective-mass systems, governed by the TDSE incorporating the BenDaniel-Duke kinetic operator, which accounts for spatially varying effective mass m(r).

**Key observables.** Tunnel splittings, High-Harmonic Generation (HHG) spectra, dipole moment, and total energy expectation values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong laser field amplitudes ($E_0$), dimensionality (1D vs 3D), and the effective mass function $m(r)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The time-propagation engine is currently CPU-optimized, and the framework relies on the weak-form formulation to handle mass-gradient singularities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of 1D vs 3D HHG spectra showing spectral differences due to dimensionality, and plots demonstrate the time evolution of energy and position expectation values confirming numerical stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the implementation of the BDD operator via weak-form Galerkin discretization, validates the static eigensolver against WKB theory, and demonstrates the time-propagation engine on benchmarks like HHG and constant-mass systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present \textsc{TDSE-Z}, a high-performance open-source framework for strong-field quantum dynamics in atomic, molecular, and semiconductor effective-mass systems. The core engine implements a weak-form Galerkin discretisation of the Hermitian BenDaniel-Duke operator, $\hat{T}_{\mathrm{BDD}} = -\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot(m^{-1}(\mathbf{r})\nabla)$, on geometry-adapted B-spline meshes, supporting arbitrary potentials and customisable laser configurations in one to three dimensions. We validate the static position-dependent-mass (PDM) eigensolver through two stringent benchmarks: a comparison to the analytical Quesne PDM model and a $\text{GaAs/Al}_{0.3}\text{Ga}_{0.7}\text{As}$ double quantum well, where the exponential decay of computed tunnel splittings follows Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin (WKB) theory at the sub-percent level. We further demonstrate the time-propagation engine on constant-mass systems, accurately reproducing high-harmonic generation (HHG) spectra in atomic benchmarks and confirming the importance of dimensionality in fully capturing the strong light-matter interaction. Our implementation demonstrates robust strong-scaling efficiency, maintaining performance across hundreds of CPU cores. While the static eigensolver currently supports optional GPU offloading, the time-propagation engine is CPU-optimised, providing a modular architecture for future expansion toward exascale quantum dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Bernstein-Vazirani Networks (BVNs), a non-variational quantum machine learning framework that uses quantum interference for supervised learning. By generalizing the standard BV algorithm, the authors create more expressive models capable of handling complex data structures without relying on gradient optimization. This offers a promising, efficient alternative to current PQC methods for quantum data processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a quantum algorithm paradigm for ML that bypasses the gradient-based optimization issues (like barren plateaus) plaguing many current quantum simulation and computation approaches.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models that overcome the architectural and trainability challenges associated with existing approaches, particularly Parametrised Quantum Circuits (PQCs).

**Main result.** The proposed Bernstein-Vazirani Networks (BVNs) achieve strong generalization capabilities and competitive performance on real-world tasks while remaining gradient-free and more sampling-efficient than PQC models.

**Method.** The framework uses quantum interference, generalizing the standard Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm by introducing task-adaptive basis functions and expressive input representations to encode complex data structures.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum circuit architecture (BVN) that processes labelled data by placing it in superposition and interfering the resulting states in a problem-adapted basis, avoiding the need for classical outer-loop optimization.

**Key observables.** Accuracy (Acc.) on classification tasks, measurement distribution sparsity, and the ability to reconstruct coefficients in closed form.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurement budget, the choice of interference operator (Hadamard, Chebyshev, Fourier), and the regularization factor $\lambda$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors address the 'leakage' problem and assume that generalizing the interference mechanism allows for universal function approximation without relying on gradient descent.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between standard and generalized BVNs for 2D point classification, showing improved decision boundaries and better fitting of target functions compared to standard models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the limitations of PQCs, detailing the standard BVN mechanism, generalizing it with task-adaptive bases, and finally testing the enhanced model on synthetic and real-world datasets (Iris, Penguins).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce Bernstein-Vazirani Networks (BVNs), a non-variational quantum machine learning framework that leverages quantum interference for supervised learning, demonstrated on vision and representation learning tasks. In their standard form, BVNs follow the principle of quantum Fourier sampling: labelled data are placed in superposition and interfered in the Fourier basis to extract globally informative features. We then define generalised BVNs that enable interference in problem-adapted bases, yielding more expressive models under the same measurement budget as in the standard setting. BVNs achieve universal function approximation through (over)complete interference bases, while training of BVNs is gradient-free. Experiments on synthetic and real-world classification tasks, as well as implicit image representation, show strong generalisation capabilities and competitive performance with classical and quantum baselines.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work examines the chiral signatures at the boundary during a quantum phase transition into a Chern insulator. By employing conformal field theory methods, the authors demonstrate that the bulk topological anomaly is precisely accounted for by delocalized chiral modes near the boundary. This provides a generalized understanding of the bulk-edge correspondence beyond simple free Dirac models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of CFT techniques to boundary anomalies in topological phases provides deep insights into how bulk topological invariants manifest at boundaries, which is crucial for understanding edge states in quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the signatures of chirality and anomaly matching at quantum phase transitions involving Chern insulators, particularly when a boundary is present.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that while the chiral edge mode delocalizes into the bulk at criticality, the boundary fermion correlation function retains a chiral structure, and the bulk Hall anomaly is matched by these delocalized chiral modes.

**Method.** The authors use a Dirac mass domain wall construction and analyze the system using the residual conformal symmetry of a (2+1)d CFT in a half-space to identify parity-odd terms in correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the transition between a trivial insulator and a Chern insulator ($C=1$), described by a massless Dirac fermion in 2+1 dimensions, and later extended to (3+1)d topological insulator transitions.

**Key observables.** Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), fermion two-point function, current and energy-momentum tensor correlation functions, and anomaly coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chern number ($C$), mass parameter ($m$), and the critical point where the bulk gap closes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on studying the transition using the domain wall picture and generalizing the framework to arbitrary time-reversal breaking (2+1)d CFTs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots the Chern number $C$ versus a lattice parameter $m$. Figure 2 illustrates mass domain wall configurations for both $C=1$ and the critical $C=1/2$ transition.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the lattice model and the domain wall construction for the $C=1$ transition, then calculating correlation functions using CFT techniques, and finally generalizing the results to higher Chern numbers and (3+1)d systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate signatures of chirality at Chern insulator transitions in the presence of a boundary. The transition between a trivial insulator and a Chern insulator with Chern number $C=1$ is described by a massless Dirac fermion whose parity anomaly gives a critical Hall conductivity $σ_{xy}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^2}{h}$. Using a Dirac mass domain wall construction, we show that the chiral edge mode delocalizes into the bulk at criticality, but the boundary fermion correlation function retains a chiral structure and acquires the scaling dimension of the bulk fermion. We compute current correlation functions and demonstrate that the anomaly of the bulk Hall response is matched by delocalized chiral modes near the boundary. Using only the residual conformal symmetry of a (2+1)d conformal field theory (CFT) in a half-space, we identify parity-odd terms in current and energy-momentum tensor correlation functions that encode these modes and determine their electromagnetic and gravitational anomaly coefficients. Our analysis therefore applies to general time-reversal breaking (2+1)d CFTs, beyond the free Dirac transition. We also extend our results to higher Chern number transitions and to the transition between a (3+1)d topological insulator and a trivial insulator.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the spin dynamics of the $S=1$ Kitaev magnet using Schwinger-boson mean-field theory. By comparing the 0-flux and $\pi/2$-flux Ansätze, the authors demonstrate that the chiral $\pi/2$-flux Ansatz provides a robust description, particularly near the pure Kitaev limit with weak perturbations. This supports the use of chiral bosonic methods for modeling spin liquid behavior in higher-spin systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed study of spin dynamics and the comparison of different mean-field Ansätze provide valuable insights into the nature of quantum spin liquid ground states, which is central to understanding strongly correlated magnetic materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to understand the complex spin dynamics in higher-spin ($S=1$) Kitaev magnets, which are not exactly solvable, by comparing different mean-field descriptions.

**Main result.** The triplet $\pi/2$-flux chiral bosonic Ansatz is supported as a useful mean-field description, showing that this flux pattern yields a flatter spectrum and better captures short-range correlations compared to the 0-flux Ansatz.

**Method.** The analysis employs Schwinger-boson mean-field theory (SBMFT) using a bond-operator formulation to calculate the dynamical spin structure factor.

**Model / system.** The system is the spin-1 Kitaev model on the honeycomb lattice, generalized to include weak symmetric off-diagonal exchanges ($\Gamma$ and $\Gamma'$).

**Key observables.** Dynamical spin structure factor $S(oldsymbol{q}, \omega)$, real-space spin correlations, and spectral flatness.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the pure Kitaev limit and weak perturbations along the line $\Gamma' = \Gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study relies on the triplet 0-flux and $\pi/2$-flux Ansätze within the SBMFT framework, and spectral flatness is noted as a consistency check, not a definitive diagnostic of chirality.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the context suggests figures comparing spectral features and correlation functions between different flux Ansätze.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the pure Kitaev limit by comparing the 0-flux and $\pi/2$-flux Ansätze. It then extends the analysis to weak off-diagonal exchanges ($\Gamma, \Gamma'$) and confirms the utility of the $\pi/2$-flux Ansatz by comparing results to finite-size exact diagonalization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Kitaev model is a paradigmatic system for realizing quantum spin liquids, but its higher-spin extensions are not exactly solvable, and their spin dynamics is less well understood than in the spin-1/2 case. In this work, we reexamine a previously introduced triplet-pairing $φ_t = π/2$ phase pattern for the antiferromagnetic $S = 1$ Kitaev model and extend the analysis to weak symmetric off-diagonal exchanges $Γ$ and $Γ'$. Using a bond-operator formulation of Schwinger-boson mean-field theory, we calculate the dynamical spin structure factor for the triplet 0-flux and triplet $π/2$-flux Ansätze with a spin-correlation scheme appropriate for Kitaev interactions. In the pure Kitaev limit, the $π/2$-flux Ansatz yields a flatter spectrum than the 0-flux Ansatz. The real-space spin correlations show that the $π/2$-flux Ansatz suppresses longer-distance correlations more strongly than the 0-flux Ansatz, yielding a correlation pattern closer to the short-ranged form expected in the Kitaev limit. This comparison shows that the flatness of $S(\boldsymbol{q}, ω)$ is tied to short-ranged spin correlations and is therefore an important consistency check, although it is not, by itself, a diagnostic of time-reversal-symmetry breaking. We then study weak off-diagonal exchanges along $Γ' = Γ$ near the pure Kitaev limit, taking the same-sign relation from analyses of candidate spin-1 Kitaev materials. Gapped solutions are obtained within the constrained $π/2$-flux manifold, and the spectra share the qualitative energy- and momentum-space features found by finite-size exact diagonalization. Taken together, these results support the triplet $π/2$-flux chiral bosonic Ansatz as a useful mean-field description of spin dynamics near the antiferromagnetic $S = 1$ Kitaev limit with weak off-diagonal exchanges.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends entanglement selection criteria from bipartite to genuine tripartite entanglement in particle scattering. By analyzing gluon and graviton scattering amplitudes, the authors demonstrate that GTE uniquely singles out the gauge-invariant and diffeomorphism-invariant theories. This establishes a non-trivial link between fundamental symmetries and multipartite quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is high-energy theory (gauge/gravity), the methodology heavily relies on advanced quantum information measures (GTE, GGM) applied to scattering states, making it relevant to theoretical quantum physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether genuine tripartite entanglement (GTE) can uniquely select fundamental physical theories, specifically identifying gauge-invariant and diffeomorphism-invariant theories in scattering processes.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that GTE, quantified by measures like the Concurrence Fill (FC) and Generalized Geometric Measure (GGM), uniquely identifies the symmetry-preserving theories as those that suppress GTE in the forward/backward scattering limits.

**Method.** The authors use angular expansions around scattering limits and calculate entanglement measures (FC, GGM) for deformed scattering amplitudes in $2+1$-particle systems.

**Model / system.** The system involves a $2+1$-particle scattering process (gluon-gluon or graviton-graviton scattering) where the interactions are parameterized by a deformation factor $k$, with $k=1$ recovering the known gauge-invariant theory.

**Key observables.** Genuine Tripartite Entanglement (GTE) quantified by Concurrence Fill (FC) and Generalized Geometric Measure (GGM); scattering amplitudes $M(k)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Deformation parameter $k$, scattering angle $	heta$, and specific color factors for gluon interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the asymptotic limits (forward and backward scattering) and assumes particle C remains spatially separated from A and B.

**Figures summary.** Figures are expected to compare entanglement measures (like concurrence) as a function of the deformation parameter $k$ across different scattering angles.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from kinematics and entanglement measure definitions to analyzing the two-particle setup before focusing on the core $2+1$-particle system analysis using GTE.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent studies have explored whether physical constants and symmetries can be selected by extremizing bipartite entanglement entropy. We extend this approach to a three-particle setting. Specifically, we consider a $2+1$-particle system in which particles $B$ and $C$ are initially entangled, and then particles $A$ and $B$ scatter without the direct participation of particle $C$ for both gluon-gluon and graviton-graviton scatterings. Using angular expansions around the forward and backward scattering limits, we analyze bipartite entanglement in the corresponding two-particle system of $A$ and $B$ and two measures of genuine tripartite entanglement (GTE), the concurrence fill and the generalized geometric measure (GGM), in the three-particle system. As is already known, the bipartite entanglement provides no universal criterion for selecting the symmetry-preserving theory: depending on the initial helicities, it may either maximize or minimize the entanglement entropy. By contrast, we find that the GTE-based analysis uniquely identifies the gauge-invariant and diffeomorphism-invariant theories as those that suppress the GTE in gluon-gluon and graviton-graviton scatterings, respectively. This result suggests a nontrivial connection between tripartite entanglement and the gauge and diffeomorphism symmetries of fundamental interactions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates 'quantum magic,' a quantum resource, by applying it to high-energy particle collisions in QED and electroweak processes. It compares the amount of magic generated when spin is projected using two different reference frames: the lab basis and the helicity basis. The findings highlight basis dependence in quantum resources and confirm the robustness of the weak mixing angle measurement across these different physical bases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of basis-dependent quantum resources like 'magic' provides a novel lens through which to view fundamental symmetries and physical constants in particle physics, potentially linking quantum information theory to high-energy phenomenology.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper quantifies 'quantum magic' (non-stabilizerness) as a resource, extending its application from quantum computing to fundamental high-energy physics processes.

**Main result.** Magic production is generally smaller in the helicity basis in the ultra-relativistic limit, while the physical weak mixing angle remains close to the magic-minimizing value in both the lab and helicity bases.

**Method.** The authors calculate the resource using the second-order Stabilizer Rényi entropy ($M_2$) and compare its value when spin is projected along the incoming beam axis (lab basis) versus the direction of particle motion (helicity basis).

**Model / system.** The study models high energy collisions involving Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) and electroweak processes (charged-lepton scattering) using spin-1/2 fermions (qubits).

**Key observables.** Quantum Magic ($M_2$), basis dependence of magic, and the value of the weak mixing angle ($\sin^2 	heta_W$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultra-relativistic limit, non-relativistic regime, and the weak mixing angle ($\sin^2 	heta_W$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of the $M_2$ measure and compares results derived from the lab basis versus the helicity basis.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the difference between the helicity and lab bases for spin projection. Figure 2 plots the non-zero angular distributions of $M_2$ generated by rotations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares magic production in the lab and helicity bases for QED and electroweak scattering processes, analyzing both ultra-relativistic and non-relativistic limits, and discussing circuit realizations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum magic, or nonstabilizerness, is a quantum resource associated with computational advantage in quantum systems. In high energy collisions, Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) is inefficient at generating magic   while the weak mixing angle, a fundamental constant of nature, sits near a value that minimizes magic production in charged-lepton scattering. These observations were made in the laboratory (lab) basis, in which spin is projected along the incoming beam axis. An alternative choice is the helicity basis, in which spin is projected along the direction of motion of each particle. The transformation between these two bases is, in general, not a Clifford operation and therefore can change the amount of magic. We present a detailed study of magic production in both bases for QED and electroweak processes, and compare these results with the basis-invariant non-local magic. In the ultra-relativistic limit, magic production is generally smaller in the helicity basis due to helicity selection rules, while the lab basis generally yields less magic in the non-relativistic regime. We provide circuit realizations of the ultra-relativistic Bhabha amplitudes using linear combinations of unitaries and show that the lab basis construction contains a larger $T$-gate count at generic scattering angles. Interestingly, in both bases the physical weak mixing angle lies close to the value that minimizes magic production.

</details>
