**Summary.** This work analyzes the role of entanglement batteries and catalysts in determining the limits of local state discrimination. It proves that while perfect discrimination of certain entangled state sets is impossible under LOCC, the authors demonstrate specific scenarios where these entanglement resources yield substantial advantages. This deepens the understanding of resource quantification in quantum information tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical bounds on what can be achieved in quantum communication tasks (state discrimination) when entanglement is treated as a consumable or reusable resource, which is fundamental to quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the theoretical limits and advantages of using entanglement batteries and entanglement catalysts in Local State Discrimination Problems (LSDPs).

**Main result.** While perfect discrimination of an orthonormal basis of entangled states is impossible using exact entanglement resources under LOCC, the authors construct nontrivial cases where entanglement batteries provide a significant advantage, especially in approximate or many-copy scenarios.

**Method.** The analysis employs quantum information theory tools, including entanglement measures (like Squashed Entanglement) and concepts like local indistinguishability, to derive necessary conditions for perfect discrimination.

**Model / system.** The model involves a composite quantum system where a state is chosen from a set $S$ and must be identified using only Local Operations and Classical Communication (LOCC). Resources include entanglement batteries and catalysts, often modeled using maximally entangled GHZ states.

**Key observables.** Entanglement measures, von Neumann entropy, and the probability of successful state discrimination or resource recovery.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cardinality of the set $|S|$, the total dimension $D$, and the number of copies $m$ used in the ensemble.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the assumption that entanglement measures are additive on tensor products, and it distinguishes between exact and approximate resource usage.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text describes constructions involving specific multipartite states and resource transformations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing no-go theorems for perfect discrimination (Theorem 1) to constructing specific, nontrivial examples where entanglement batteries provide advantages, covering both exact and approximate resource regimes, and finally addressing the many-copy limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we study the limitations and advantages of using entanglement battery and entanglement catalyst in local state discrimination problems. We consider both cases of such tools, i.e., exact and approximate cases. We show that to distinguish any set of orthogonal pure bipartite entangled states perfectly under local operations and classical communication using an (exact) entanglement battery or an (exact) entanglement catalyst, it is necessary to consider that the cardinality of the set must be smaller than the total dimension of the given Hilbert space. Then, we construct a nontrivial case where an exact entanglement battery can provide huge advantage. We also construct other nontrivial cases where exact or approximate entanglement battery or entanglement catalyst can be useful. In fact, we find that the approximate tools are particularly useful in the local discrimination of certain sets which can be derived from many-copy indistinguishable ensembles.

</details>

(a) At the previous time step, a measurement error misidentified the vacuum as an a anyon. This configuration is analogous to standard spacetime matching of defects in the Abelian Z2 TO [14]. (b) An a anyon was not identified at the previous time step due to a measurement error and then fused with another a anyon created by a physical error at time t, leading to a probabilistic syndrome measurement at (s, t) that yields...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an Integer Linear Programming (ILP) decoder to solve the minimum-weight error detection problem for arbitrary topological codes. By encoding complex anyon fusion rules as linear constraints, the ILP decoder overcomes the limitations of simpler matching or clustering decoders. It demonstrates superior performance across Abelian and non-Abelian topological orders, establishing ILP as a powerful general tool for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework provided by ILP offers a powerful, general tool for analyzing fault-tolerance thresholds in complex quantum systems beyond simple nearest-neighbor models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a general-purpose, high-performance decoder for topological codes (TOs) that can handle arbitrary fusion rules and correlated noise, surpassing limitations of existing matching-based and clustering decoders.

**Main result.** The Integer Linear Programming (ILP) decoder is shown to provide a clear performance advantage over existing methods for various TOs, including $\mathbb{Z}_2$, $\mathbb{Z}_3$, and non-Abelian $D_4$ systems.

**Method.** The error-correction problem is formulated as an ILP, linearizing the constraints by introducing auxiliary variables to encode complex anyon fusion rules and correlations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Topological Orders (TOs) used as quantum error-correcting codes, tested on Abelian ($\mathbb{Z}_2, \mathbb{Z}_3$) and non-Abelian ($D_4$) systems.

**Key observables.** Error syndromes ($\sigma$), error configurations ($E$), and the error-correction threshold.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The noise model (e.g., depolarizing noise) and the specific topological order being analyzed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ILP formulation requires careful construction of variables and constraints; some analyses use big-M relaxations or assume certain error loop configurations are rare.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare error-correction thresholds for $\mathbb{Z}_2$ and $\mathbb{Z}_3$ TOs, demonstrating the ILP decoder's superior performance compared to other decoding methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the ILP framework, applies it to Abelian TOs ($\mathbb{Z}_2, \mathbb{Z}_3$) to establish general applicability, and then extends the method to the non-Abelian $D_4$ TO, detailing the multi-step decoding process.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological orders (TOs) are widely used as quantum error-correcting codes, with anyon excitations serving as error syndromes. For certain Abelian TOs, decoding can be performed by independently matching particle-antiparticle pairs of each species. However, matching-based decoders cannot handle more general fusion rules in either Abelian or non-Abelian TOs, nor account for noise that correlates different anyon species. While clustering decoders are more broadly applicable, they typically neglect anyon data and fusion properties, leading to poor performance in practice. In this work, we introduce a fundamentally different decoder for arbitrary TOs based on integer linear programming (ILP). The ILP formulation linearizes the error-correction problem through the introduction of auxiliary variables and encodes fusion rules as linear constraints. Classical optimization then identifies the minimum-weight error configuration. As concrete examples, we determine error-correction thresholds for three TOs: the Abelian $\mathbb{Z}_2$ TO under depolarizing noise, where charge and flux errors are correlated; the Abelian $\mathbb{Z}_3$ TO, which does not admit a pairwise matching decoder; and the non-Abelian $D_4$ TO under noise channels that generate all anyon species. We demonstrate the versatility of the ILP decoder by showing a clear performance advantage over most existing decoders in all three cases. We further extend the method to incorporate noisy syndrome measurements and propose a just-in-time variant for continuous error correction. Our results establish ILP as a natural framework for handling correlated errors and general anyon fusion rules, and as a powerful and flexible general-purpose decoder for incoherent anyon noise in arbitrary TOs, with applications to fault-tolerant quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how well algorithms perform on hard optimization problems like MIS and Max K-SAT, moving beyond worst-case asymptotic guarantees. It demonstrates that even when theory predicts failure in the limit, sophisticated algorithms can achieve surprisingly good results in finite, intermediate regimes. This highlights the continued practical importance of algorithmic design even when asymptotic theory suggests otherwise.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of algorithmic performance on highly constrained random systems, particularly the gap between finite-size and asymptotic behavior, is highly relevant to understanding the complexity of optimization problems encountered in quantum simulation and machine learning models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the algorithmic performance of solving hard, NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, such as Maximum Independent Set (MIS) and Max K-SAT.

**Main result.** While asymptotic theory predicts that no polynomial-time algorithm significantly outperforms heuristics in the limit, the study shows that convergence to these bounds can be remarkably slow, especially in the intermediate constraint regime.

**Method.** The analysis combines rigorous average-case asymptotic analysis (using density evolution and population dynamics) with the study of finite-size behavior of various local and greedy algorithms.

**Model / system.** The primary models are random graphs, specifically sparse Erdős-Rényi graphs $G(n, \lambda/n)$ and graphs derived from the configuration model, used to represent the constraints of the optimization problems.

**Key observables.** Performance ratios (e.g., $\sigma_A(G) / \sigma(G)$) comparing the algorithm's output size to the true maximum value ($\sigma(G)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph size ($n$), mean degree ($\lambda$), and the constraint density regime (easy, intermediate, hard).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on asymptotic limits ($n 	o \infty$) and approximations like Population Dynamics for message-passing algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the average performance ratio of different algorithms (DG, SDG, MP) versus the mean degree $\lambda$. Figure 2 quantifies the convergence rate of the SDG algorithm towards a theoretical threshold.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the negative results in the double asymptotic limit. It then revisits the problem by analyzing finite-size behavior, applying techniques like density evolution and population dynamics to MIS and Max K-SAT to demonstrate slow convergence in intermediate regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hard combinatorial optimization problems, many of which are NP-hard, present fundamental algorithmic challenges. Average-case analysis on random instances has emerged as a powerful framework for understanding typical algorithmic performance beyond worst-case guarantees. A substantial body of work has established negative results: for sufficiently hard instances (often controlled by the underlying graph connectivity/constraints density), no known polynomial-time algorithm can significantly outperform naive heuristics in the double asymptotic limit where both problem size and constraints density tend to infinity. We revisit this picture by studying the finite-size behavior of some optimization algorithms across easy, intermediate, and hard regimes. Through rigorous analysis of large-graph asymptotics combined with numerical experiments on canonical problems (maximum independent set and maximum $K$-SAT), we demonstrate that while algorithms do eventually converge to theoretically predicted bounds, this convergence can be remarkably slow. In the intermediate regime where instances are already highly constrained, local algorithms achieve solutions substantially better than their predicted performance in the high-constraint-density limit. This gap between finite-regime and asymptotic behavior has important practical implications: sophisticated algorithmic design remains crucial even when asymptotic theory predicts inevitable failure.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes new quantum-geometric bounds governing the Casimir force between 2D topological materials. It shows that these bounds constrain the force's sign and magnitude, suggesting that the repulsion onset occurs at shorter distances than previously predicted. The findings offer new optimization strategies for observing repulsive Casimir forces in materials like twisted TMDs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work connects topological invariants (Chern numbers) and geometric properties (quantum metric) directly to macroscopic forces like the Casimir effect, providing new theoretical constraints for condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives new quantum-geometric bounds on the magnitude and sign of the Casimir force between two-dimensional plates in the long-distance limit.

**Main result.** The derived bounds show that increasing the Chern number can be counterproductive for maximizing repulsion, and the true crossover to repulsion occurs at shorter, more experimentally accessible distances than the bounds suggest.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Lifshitz scattering formula, applying quantum geometric bounds derived from the material's DC response (like conductivity and polarizability) to constrain the Casimir energy.

**Model / system.** The system involves two-dimensional insulating plates, specifically modeling Chern insulators, Landau levels, and twisted bilayer materials like MoTe2.

**Key observables.** Casimir force ($F/A$), crossover distance ($d_F$), dielectric susceptibility, and Hall conductivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Separation distance ($d$), Chern numbers ($C_1, C_2$), and the effective Bohr radius ($a^*_\perp$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The bounds are derived using the leading large-distance approximation, which is noted to be overly conservative compared to full finite-frequency calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the calculated Casimir energy/force for various models (Landau levels, Chern insulators) against the derived geometric bounds, showing discrepancies in the crossover distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Casimir energy calculation using the Lifshitz formula, separates the energy into dielectric and Hall terms, derives the quantum-geometric bounds, and then benchmarks these bounds against exact results from specific physical models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum geometric tensor has been shown to bound the gap, optical absorption, and dielectric susceptibilities of materials. Here we derive new quantum-geometric bounds on the magnitude and sign of the Casimir force between two-dimensional plates in the long-distance limit. These bounds limit the previously attributed benefit of increasing the plate's Chern number to maximize repulsion, and give a quantum geometric origin to the stronger attractive force of metallic plates, regardless of their Chern number. These bounds allow us to infer that flat Chern bands that saturate geometric bounds, including Landau levels and moiré flat bands, enlarge the window where Casimir repulsion exists and bring the repulsive crossover to smaller, more experimentally relevant distances. We derive estimates for material platforms such as twisted MoTe$_2$. Our work shows that quantum-geometric bounds constrain repulsive Casimir forces beyond previously known theorems, and suggests new optimization strategies to observe repulsion.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the adjacent gap ratio of singular values of the fermion matrix as a robust diagnostic for phase transitions in DQMC simulations. By analyzing Hubbard models, the authors demonstrate that this spectral statistic remains sensitive to underlying physics even when the simulation encounters a sign or phase problem. This establishes a powerful, sign-problem-resilient probe for strongly correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a powerful, purely spectral diagnostic tool that bypasses the fundamental sign problem inherent in simulating fermionic quantum many-body systems, which is crucial for understanding correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The sign problem limits the use of determinant quantum Monte Carlo (DQMC) for studying strongly correlated fermion systems, necessitating a sign-problem-resilient diagnostic for phase transitions.

**Main result.** The adjacent gap ratio of low-lying singular values of the space-time fermion matrix successfully tracks interaction-driven phase transitions in both sign-free and complex-weight Hubbard models, remaining robust against phase reweighting.

**Method.** The authors use the adjacent gap ratio of low-lying singular values ($	ext{err}$) of the space-time fermion matrix, derived from the Hubbard-Stratonovich decoupling, as a diagnostic tool.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on half-filled honeycomb-lattice Hubbard models, including the sign-free and complex-weight Haldane-Hubbard models, analyzed via DQMC simulations.

**Key observables.** Adjacent gap ratio of low-lying singular values ($	ext{err} = \min(\delta_n, \delta_{n+1}) / \max(\delta_n, \delta_{n+1}$)), and the average phase $\langle e^{i	heta} angle_{	ext{ref}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U/t_1$), lattice geometry (honeycomb), and the auxiliary-field coupling strength ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the local spectral correlations of singular values, which are assumed to be insensitive to the sign/phase of the determinant itself.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the gap ratio $\langle 	ext{err} angle_{L	au}$ for synthetic ensembles comparing different paths and models, and also compare the gap ratio for the spin-channel versus the SU(2) charge channel.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the diagnostic using the gap ratio, applies it to the honeycomb and Haldane-Hubbard models to demonstrate transition tracking, and contrasts this spin-channel response with an SU(2) charge-channel decomposition to validate the specificity of the spin-based probe.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The sign problem limits determinant quantum Monte Carlo studies of strongly correlated fermion systems. In the spin-channel Hubbard-Stratonovich decoupling, spin correlations are exactly related to auxiliary-field correlations. This relation implies that an antiferromagnetic transition reorganizes auxiliary-field configurations and thereby changes the statistical structure of the resulting fermion matrices. We use the adjacent gap ratio of low-lying singular values of the space-time fermion matrix to probe interaction-driven transitions in two half-filled honeycomb-lattice Hubbard models. In the sign-free honeycomb Hubbard model, the statistic tracks the established transition from a Dirac semimetal to an antiferromagnetic Mott insulator. In the complex-weight Haldane-Hubbard model, the transition-sensitive feature remains visible in the phase-quenched reference ensemble and occurs near previous estimates of the transition. Moreover, phase reweighting only weakly modifies the gap ratio over the regimes investigated, despite the rapid suppression of the average phase. These results establish singular-value statistics as a sign-problem-resilient probe of interaction-driven transitions in determinant quantum Monte Carlo.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates ultrasonic attenuation in inhomogeneous superconductors to probe the nature of subgap bound states. By using a BdG formulation, the authors show that the attenuation signal is highly sensitive to the spin structure and symmetry of the bound states. This allows ultrasound to distinguish between different types of electronic states, such as those induced by non-magnetic versus magnetic impurities at grain boundaries.

**Why it may be interesting.** The sensitivity of the attenuation to spin structure and symmetry provides a powerful, non-spectroscopic probe for understanding exotic pairing symmetries and local magnetic moments in strongly correlated superconductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how ultrasonic attenuation can be used to distinguish between different types of superconducting bound states (e.g., magnetic vs. non-magnetic) in inhomogeneous superconductors.

**Main result.** The attenuation signature is highly sensitive to the symmetry and spin structure of the bound states; specifically, local magnetism can induce a clear attenuation peak where non-magnetic impurities cause only weak signals due to coherence factor cancellation.

**Method.** The authors use a real-space Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) formulation combined with linear response theory to calculate the first-order correction to the phonon decay rate.

**Model / system.** The study models superconductors, focusing on inhomogeneous junctions like phase-biased SNS junctions and grain boundaries in $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductors. The calculation involves the electron-phonon interaction derived from hopping parameter variations.

**Key observables.** Ultrasonic attenuation coefficient ($\alpha(q, \lambda)$) and the phonon lifetime ($	au_{q\lambda}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures, phonon frequency matching subgap level separation, and the presence/absence of local magnetism.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the first-order correction in the decay rate calculation and uses an effective mean-field model for the superconducting gap.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate gap structures, the comparison of attenuation from non-magnetic vs. magnetic bound states, and the temperature dependence of attenuation in s-wave and d-wave systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using BdG formalism and linear response theory, applies it to specific inhomogeneous geometries (SNS junctions, grain boundaries), and contrasts the resulting attenuation signatures based on the nature of the impurity/disorder.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Subgap bound states can produce pronounced features in the density of states without necessarily giving rise to a corresponding signal in ultrasonic attenuation. We show that the coherence factors act as a filter for the contribution of bound states to ultrasonic attenuation. In a phase-biased superconductor--normal--superconductor junction, the Andreev bound states produce a resonance in the attenuation when the phonon frequency matches the phase-dependent separation between the subgap levels. The situation is different at a $(110)$ grain boundary in a $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductor. There, nonmagnetic impurity-induced bound states remain weak in the attenuation despite their large spectral weight, because the relevant coherence factors nearly cancel. When local magnetism develops near the boundary, this cancellation is removed, the bound states become spin split, and a clear attenuation peak appears, which we argue is governed by a process involving emission of phonons by pair of quasiparticles at low temperatures. We obtain these results using a real-space Bogoliubov--de Gennes formulation of ultrasonic attenuation and benchmark the method against the known low-temperature behavior of homogeneous $s$- and $d$-wave superconductors. The results show that ultrasound can distinguish bound states with similar spectral signatures but different symmetry and spin structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a comprehensive theoretical framework to classify unconventional Charge-Density Wave (CDW) orders on hexagonal lattices by integrating local symmetry constraints (little group) with multiple ordering wave vectors. Using RPA on the triangular lattice, the authors derive Landau free energies whose coefficients determine whether the ground state favors a single-momentum-vector (single-Q) or multi-momentum-vector (multi-Q) order. This provides a systematic route for predicting exotic electronic ground states in correlated quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of group theory to classify complex ordered phases like CDWs provides a powerful, generalizable theoretical tool for understanding electronic instabilities in correlated materials, which is relevant to understanding exotic quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a systematic framework to describe unconventional Charge-Density Wave (CDW) phases that incorporate both local symmetries (little group) and multiple symmetry-related ordering wave vectors (the 'star').

**Main result.** The stability of the CDW state (single-Q vs. multi-Q) is determined by the sign of the coefficient $2eta_1 - eta_2$ in the Landau free energy, allowing for a systematic classification of ground states.

**Method.** The analysis combines group-theoretical symmetry analysis (using little groups and space groups) with mean-field theory, specifically calculating Landau free energy coefficients via the Random Phase Approximation (RPA).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-dimensional hexagonal lattices, using the triangular lattice as a minimal example, analyzed via a tight-binding model and Hubbard-type interactions.

**Key observables.** Order parameters ($\Delta, \psi, \eta$), Landau free energy coefficients ($\alpha, eta, \gamma$), and the resulting CDW structure (bond or current density).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemical potential ($\mu$), temperature ($T$), and the relative signs of the quartic free energy coefficients ($eta_1, eta_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field approximations (RPA) and the systematic incorporation of local symmetries via the little group.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hexagonal Brillouin zone and the classification of form factors based on symmetry. Other figures show the dependence of free energy coefficients ($eta_1, 2eta_1 + eta_2$) on temperature and chemical potential, determining the phase stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the general symmetry framework (LG/SG) for unconventional CDWs, deriving the relevant form factors and symmetry-constrained Landau free energies, and finally applying this framework to the triangular lattice using RPA calculations to determine the ground state phase.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Charge-density wave (CDW) orders are conventionally described as modulations of on-site charge at an ordering wave vector $\boldsymbol{Q}$ with symmetry-related wave vectors typically forming a multicomponent order-parameter manifold. Recent developments significantly broadened this phenomenology to bond and loop-current density waves, which possess nontrivial, potentially symmetry-breaking textures within the unit cell in addition to their spatial modulation at $\boldsymbol{Q}$. Such textures arise from particle-hole condensates with nonzero angular momentum, analogous to unconventional superconductivity, and their symmetries are described by the little group $G_{\boldsymbol{Q}}$. In this work, we develop a framework for unconventional CDW phases that simultaneously incorporates the local symmetries described by little group and the presence of multiple symmetry-related ordering wave vectors, also known as the star. The resulting multicomponent order parameter transforms under representations of the full space group induced from irreducible representations of $G_{\boldsymbol{Q}}$. We apply this framework to two-dimensional lattices with sixfold symmetry and ordering wave vectors along high-symmetry lines, and derive the corresponding Landau free energies. As a microscopic example, we demonstrate the emergence of unconventional bond and loop-current orders from electronic interactions on the triangular lattice within the random phase approximation, and determine their ground states by microscopically evaluating the relevant coefficients in the free energy. Our framework provides a systematic route to describing unconventional modulated phases and can be readily extended to more complex lattices.

</details>

### [(Almost) quadruply optimal unitary designs in 1D](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18650v1)

**Authors:** Guoding Liu, Jonas Helsen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18650v1>

The con- struction employs a two-layer block-brickwork architecture acting on a 1D chain, where each block spans 2ξ qubits with an overlap of ξ qubits between adjacent layers. The block size scales as ξ = O(log(n/ε) + k). Each 2ξ-qubit block is realized as a magic-augmented circuit, consisting of a random Clifford operation flanked by ℓ-local ε′′-error approximate unitary k-designs, where the locality is ℓ= O(log k) and the relative error of the local block is set to ε′′ = 2−2ℓk.</figcaption>

An ℓ-qubit approximate unitary k-design with relative error ε′′ is constructed by repeating O(ℓk + log(1/ε′′)) rounds of CPZPC generators. Each individual round consists of 2rC Clifford group generators, 2rP permutation group generators, and a single Pauli Z gate, where rC and rP are fixed constants. All generators are strictly constant-local in a 1D nearest-neighbor layout.</figcaption>
</figure>

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper presents a method to construct approximate unitary k-designs on 1D quantum hardware that achieves near-optimal scaling for both circuit depth and the number of required magic gates. By refining previous techniques and combining them with the Gluing Lemma, the authors demonstrate efficient resource utilization for generating highly mixed quantum states on linear quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, resource-efficient circuit constructions for achieving highly mixed quantum states (unitary designs) on physically constrained 1D hardware, which is crucial for quantum simulation and computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is constructing approximate unitary k-designs on 1D quantum architectures with minimal resource overhead regarding circuit depth and magic gates.

**Main result.** The authors achieve simultaneous near-optimality in circuit depth and T-gate count for 1D systems, matching known lower bounds up to a logarithmic factor.

**Method.** The construction combines and refines existing results, utilizing a two-layer block-brickwork architecture and the Gluing Lemma to extend local designs to global ones.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a 1D nearest-neighbor quantum architecture (qubits) implementing unitary operations, involving Clifford group operations and random permutations.

**Key observables.** Circuit depth, number of magic gates (T gates), and the relative error ($\epsilon$) of the unitary k-design.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Design order $k$, system size $n$, relative error $\epsilon$, and locality $\ell$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the Gluing Lemma and assumes the block size is an integer multiple of the locality parameter.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the two-layer block-brickwork circuit architecture for the design, and Figure 2 relates to implementing the local generators using CPZPC generators.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first improving the magic block size reduction, then constructing a constant-spectral-gap generating set for the Clifford group, and finally combining these elements using the gluing lemma to prove the main near-optimal design result.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct $n$-qubit approximate unitary $k$-designs in 1D systems, achieving circuit depth $O(\log(n/\varepsilon) + k\log k)$ with relative error $\varepsilon$ and requiring $O(nk\log k)$ magic gates. This matches existing lower bounds $Ω(\log(n/\varepsilon) + k)$ for circuit depth, and $\widetildeΩ(nk)$ for the required number of $T$ gates, up to a $\log k$ factor, achieving simultaneous near-optimality in all parameters. Our construction is based on a combination and refinement of two existing results. We reduce the required magic block size for breaking Clifford symmetries in the magic-augmented circuit construction of Zhang et al. from $O(k\log k)$ to $O(\log k)$. We also improve the breakthrough construction of Chen et al. to construct a generating set of 1D local constant-depth circuits for the unitary group with a constant spectral gap, making $O(\log k)$-local random unitaries realizable in depth $O(k\log k)$. As a by-product, we provide a constant-size 1D-local generating set for the Clifford group, which we expect to be of independent interest. Combining the two results with the gluing lemma, we prove the final result.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces AlphaClifford, a novel model-based Reinforcement Learning framework that uses Monte Carlo Tree Search to efficiently synthesize and optimize Clifford quantum circuits. By modeling the problem using the algebraic structure of the symplectic group, it significantly outperforms existing heuristics in minimizing gate counts for both general optimization and hardware-constrained transpilation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract computation, the rigorous mathematical modeling of the state space using group theory (symplectic group) and the application of advanced AI search techniques (MCTS) provide powerful computational tools applicable to modeling complex quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard methods for synthesizing Clifford quantum circuits yield sub-optimal gate counts, which is inefficient for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** AlphaClifford achieves significant reductions (up to 56% total gate reduction) in gate counts compared to state-of-the-art heuristics for both unconstrained optimization and hardware-constrained transpilation.

**Method.** The authors introduce AlphaClifford, a model-based Reinforcement Learning framework utilizing Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to explore the combinatorial space of Clifford circuits.

**Model / system.** The system involves Clifford circuits, which are mathematically characterized by their action on the symplectic group $	ext{Sp}(2n, 	ext{F}_2)$ via the symplectic matrix representation $M = [X Z]$.

**Key observables.** Total gate count and two-qubit (CNOT) gate count are minimized; the goal is to match a target symplectic matrix $M_{	ext{target}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fundamental gate set is {H, S, CNOT}; the state space is modeled using the algebraic properties of the symplectic group.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The synthesis guarantees equivalence only up to a Pauli layer ($P \in P_n$), relying on the homomorphism $\phi: C_n 	o 	ext{Sp}(2n, 	ext{F}_2)$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the process: (a) Training uses MCTS on the symplectic matrix tree; (b) Synthesis uses the model to find a circuit matching a target matrix; (c) Shows applications in optimization, transpilation, and logical synthesis.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the mathematical formulation using symplectic matrices, introduces the AlphaClifford RL framework powered by MCTS, presents results across general optimization and transpilation tasks, and discusses future directions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Clifford circuits play a foundational role in quantum computing, particularly due to their importance in quantum error correction and fault-tolerant logical synthesis. While these circuits can be efficiently simulated and represented as symplectic matrices, standard synthesis methods-such as the Aaronson-Gottesman algorithm-often yield sub-optimal circuits with excessively high gate counts. In this work, we introduce AlphaClifford, a model-based Reinforcement Learning framework powered by Monte Carlo Tree Search, designed to efficiently synthesize Clifford circuits from the fundamental gate set composed of H, S, and CNOT. By modeling the state space through the algebraic properties of the symplectic group, AlphaClifford effectively explores this combinatorial space to minimize overall circuit cost. For unconstrained Clifford optimization, our approach achieves a consistent reduction in both total and two-qubit (CNOT) gate counts compared to state-of-the-art synthesis heuristics, despite operating with a strictly less expressive gate set. Furthermore, we demonstrate the broad applicability of our framework on two additional tasks: hardware-constrained Clifford transpilation, where we outperform existing RL-based compilers, and as a post-synthesis optimization component within a full Clifford+T logical synthesis pipeline. Our results underscore that model-based RL is highly effective at addressing the combinatorial complexities of quantum compilation, offering a scalable pathway to mitigate hardware constraints in both near-term and future fault-tolerant quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the concept of anti-spin Laue groups to systematically classify anti-altermagnets, which exhibit unique odd-parity spin splitting. By developing this classification, the authors create a unified theoretical framework that allows for the construction of minimal, material-oriented four-band models for unconventional magnets. This advances the understanding of how magnetic symmetry dictates electronic band structure in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, symmetry-enforced framework for understanding magnetic electronic structure, which is crucial for designing novel topological or correlated electronic materials relevant to quantum phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To classify and develop minimal theoretical models for anti-altermagnets, which exhibit odd-parity nonrelativistic spin splitting and lack a simple symmetry classification analogous to existing spin Laue groups.

**Main result.** The authors introduce anti-spin Laue groups, classifying 21 distinct groups that, together with spin Laue groups, provide a complete symmetry description for unconventional magnets with collinear momentum-space spin polarization.

**Method.** The classification is achieved by projecting the full spin group onto the action on the out-of-plane spin polarization component, leading to a systematic model-construction algorithm for minimal four-band Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The focus is on anti-altermagnets, which are coplanar magnets characterized by odd-parity nonrelativistic spin splitting. The theoretical framework unifies the symmetry description for both odd- and even-parity unconventional magnets.

**Key observables.** The perpendicular spin polarization component, $S_{\perp}(k)$, and the resulting symmetry-enforced nodal spin-splitting character.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Odd-parity spin splitting, collinear momentum-space spin polarization, and the structure of the anti-spin Laue groups ($	ext{R}_{	ext{Is}}, 	ext{R}_{	ext{IIs}}, 	ext{R}_{	ext{IIIs}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction retains only the action of the spin symmetry on the collinear momentum-space spin polarization, and an effective 'anti-inversion' symmetry must be added for anti-spin Laue groups.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the relationship between different real-space lattice models belonging to the same anti-spin Laue group, showing common nodal structures in momentum space, and visualizing the perpendicular spin polarization $S_{\perp}$ and nodal planes for various crystal systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for anti-spin Laue groups due to the unique symmetry of anti-altermagnets. It then details the construction of these groups, provides a comprehensive classification (21 groups), and concludes by presenting a systematic algorithm to build minimal, material-oriented four-band models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anti-altermagnets exhibit odd-parity nonrelativistic spin splitting, yet unlike even-parity altermagnets, their momentum-space symmetries lack a reduced classification analogous to spin Laue groups. Here, we introduce anti-spin Laue groups, organized into three distinct classes, and identify 21 groups describing the odd-parity partial wave character of this unconventional class. Together with the 10 spin Laue groups of altermagnets, they complete the classification of nonrelativistic unconventional magnets with collinear momentum-space spin polarization. Anti-spin Laue groups also provide a many-to-one reduction of spin space (point) groups by retaining only their action on the collinear momentum-space spin polarization, thereby directly encoding the symmetry-enforced nodal spin-splitting character. Based on this we develop a systematic model-construction algorithm yielding minimal, material-oriented four-band models. This framework places odd- and even-parity unconventional magnets on equal footing within a unified momentum-space symmetry description.

</details>

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the rigorous mathematical framework developed for handling correlations in quantum information security has parallels with understanding complex, non-independent interactions in many-body quantum systems, where local measurements might reveal global correlations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the theoretical challenge of defining and proving security for quantum copy-protection and single-decryptor encryption (SDE) under natural, correlated challenge distributions, moving beyond simple independent challenges.

**Main result.** The authors establish plain-model security for correlated challenge unclonable puncturable obfuscation (UPO) and achieve the first provable identical-challenge copy protection for point functions and general puncturable functionalities.

**Method.** The methodology involves strengthening security definitions (e.g., correlated challenge security) and utilizing advanced cryptographic techniques, including a new decisional monogamy theorem for coset states, and relying on assumptions like post-quantum iO and quantum-hard LWE.

**Model / system.** The work is purely theoretical, dealing with abstract cryptographic primitives applied to quantum states and computational circuits. The model involves quantum states, challenge samplers, and polynomial-size keyed circuits ($	ext{Circ}$).

**Key observables.** Security notions such as correlated challenge security, identical-challenge security, and the success probability of distinguishing real vs. simulated quantum states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Security parameter $\lambda$, and the min-entropy $	ext{eH}_\infty(D\lambda)$ of the challenge sampler.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results assume the existence of post-quantum indistinguishability obfuscation (iO), one-way functions, and the quantum hardness of LWE.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (The notes refer to general concepts and theorems rather than specific figure contents).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining stronger security notions for SDE and UPO to handle correlations, then proving the existence of constructions satisfying these notions using advanced cryptographic machinery, and finally applying these results to specific functions like point functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Copy-protection encodes a functionality in a reusable quantum state that cannot be split into two states (freeloader adversaries) which remain simultaneously useful. Prior plain-model results handle only independently sampled challenges; the more natural identical-challenge notion, also tied to unclonable bits and copy-protection of point functions, has remained open. We strengthen these definitions and prove plain-model security for our new stronger notions. For single-decryptor encryption (SDE) we define correlated challenge security, show it implies all previous SDE notions including identical-challenge security, and prove that the construction of Kitagawa and Yamakawa (TCC'25) achieves it assuming iO and one-way functions. We also nearly fully characterize the relations among prior SDE notions. For general functionalities we define correlated challenge unclonable puncturable obfuscation (UPO), allowing arbitrary correlations among challenge points and puncturing bits plus auxiliary information before and after splitting, and requiring only conditionally uniform bits and $λ^c$ average conditional min-entropy in each point separately (thus, in particular, the points may be identical). Assuming post-quantum iO and quantum-hard LWE, we construct correlated UPO for polynomial-size keyed circuits with input length at least $λ^c$, answering an open question of Ananth, Behera, Huang, Kitagawa, Yamakawa (EUROCRYPT'26) and of Cakan-Goyal (EUROCRYPT'26). We also obtain the first plain-model copy protection for point functions, $k$-point functions, and compute-and-compare programs, and identical-challenge copy protection for general puncturable functionalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes a quantitative measure called 'entropic rigidity depth' ($r$) to characterize the performance gap between two decoding strategies (MP vs. MLD) in quantum error correction. By relating this depth to the code's geometry and algebra, the authors provide a universal benchmark for understanding the fundamental limits of quantum memory reliability under noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QEC, the rigorous mathematical framework for quantifying 'entropic rigidity' provides a general tool for understanding how geometric constraints (like topology or algebraic structure) impose fundamental limits on information processing capacity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the minimum physical error weight ($m$) at which the decision criteria from Maximum-Probability (MP) decoding and Degenerate Maximum-Likelihood (MLD) decoding diverge for quantum error-correcting codes.

**Main result.** The divergence onset is governed by an entropic rigidity depth $r$, leading to a quantifiable hierarchy ($r=0, 1, 2$) that provides an exact benchmark for low-noise decoder selection.

**Method.** The authors compare the peak probability (MP) against the total probability mass (MLD) using algebraic combinatorics and analyzing the lexicographic spectra of logical sectors.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes quantum error-correcting codes, including planar surface codes, toric codes, and qLDPC codes, subjected to code-capacity Pauli noise.

**Key observables.** Entropic rigidity depth ($r$), minimum error weight ($m$), and the gap $\Delta(t)$ between MP and MLD performance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), physical noise strength ($t$), and the universal bound $h = \lceil d/2 ceil$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of code-capacity Pauli noise to isolate intrinsic entropy effects and relies on defining the winner sets via set-valued comparisons.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare MP vs. MLD performance, illustrate the entropic rigidity depth ($r$), and show finite noise curves and validity windows for planar and toric geometries.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the statistical comparison between MP and MLD, deriving the entropic rigidity depth $r$ via the relation $m=h+r$, and then certifying this depth for specific families of quantum codes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maximum-probability (MP) decoding selects the most probable microscopic error, whereas degenerate maximum-likelihood (MLD) decoding includes the configurational entropy of an entire logical sector. Using code-capacity Pauli noise to isolate rigidity intrinsic to the code, we determine the first physical error weight $m$ at which their logical winner sets become disjoint, even under optimal MP tie resolution. Code distance imposes the universal bound $m\geq h=\lceil d/2\rceil$. We define the entropic rigidity depth $r$ through $m=h+r$ and certify a three-level hierarchy: $r=0$ for planar surface codes and two concatenated families, $r=1$ for odd-distance square toric codes and the Gross $[[144,12,12]]$ quantum low-density-parity-check code, and $r=2$ for a separable family with hypergraph product and bivariate bicycle descriptions. The onset fixes the leading operational failure gap, proportional to the $m$th power of the physical noise strength. Geometry and algebra therefore provide quantifiable controls of configurational entropy and an exact benchmark for low-noise decoder selection.

</details>

### [Good Stabilizer Codes from Shallow Clifford Circuits with Random Matchings](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18536v1)

**Authors:** Emile Anand, Elia Gorokhovsky, Jennifer Hritz, Jingtong Sun  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18536v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">

<img src="2608.18536_figures/2608.18536_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 2: Two Clifford-twirled CNOT matching layers. Each 𝐿(𝑡) 𝑖 , 𝑅(𝑡) 𝑖 ∈C1 is an independent one-qubit Clifford. Layer Γ(1) CNOT uses O = {(1, 2), (3, 4), (5, 6)}, while layer Γ(2) CNOT uses O = {(1, 6), (2, 5), (3, 4)}.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method to construct high-performance quantum error-correcting codes using shallow random circuits. By employing a random matching circuit architecture, the authors prove that the optimal rate-distance tradeoff can be achieved with a circuit depth scaling only logarithmically with the number of qubits. This advances the feasibility of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on shallow circuits and achieving fundamental bounds like the GV tradeoff is central to building fault-tolerant quantum computers, which is a major area of theoretical quantum physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is constructing quantum error-correcting codes with minimal circuit depth while achieving the optimal quantum Gilbert-Varshamov rate-distance tradeoff.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that random matching circuits can achieve the optimal rate-distance tradeoff with a depth of only $O(\log n)$, significantly improving upon previous results.

**Method.** The construction uses a random matching circuit architecture over $T$ layers, analyzing the resulting code properties by reducing the dynamics to a reversible Markov chain on binary support strings.

**Model / system.** The system involves encoding quantum information on $n$ qubits using random Clifford circuits composed of layers where qubits are paired by a random perfect matching, and a random two-qubit Clifford gate is applied to each pair.

**Key observables.** Code parameters include the length $n$, dimension $k$, and minimum distance $d$; the depth $T$ is a key observable.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rate-distance tradeoff parameters ($k/n$ vs $d/n$), the depth $T=O(\log n)$, and the required regularity conditions on the gate distribution $p_2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the gate distribution satisfies three regularity conditions (bi-invariance, positive entanglement, reversibility) and relies on bounding the hitting time of the associated Markov chain.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem, presents the random matching circuit model, analyzes the coding properties using Markov chain theory (hitting time bounds), and finally proves the rate-distance tradeoff by showing the depth $O(\log n)$ is sufficient.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Encoding quantum information with low circuit overhead is a fundamental challenge in fault-tolerant quantum computation. Random circuits provide a natural mechanism for rapidly spreading logical information through simple gates applied in parallel. Brown and Fawzi showed that random Clifford circuits on two-qubit Clifford gates provide such encoders that achieve the quantum Gilbert-Varshamov rate-distance tradeoff with depth $O(\log^3 n)$. We show that the same asymptotic tradeoff is attained in optimal $O(\log n)$ depth under a gate distribution with a more restricted support. For every fixed $δ>0$ and sufficiently large $n$, if $\frac kn < 1 - H(\frac{d}{n}) - \frac{d}{n}\log_2 3 - δ$, we can construct random circuits of depth $O(\log n)$ which define, with high probability, an $[n,k]$ stabilizer code of distance at least $d+1$, which matches the $Ω(\log n)$ light-cone lower bound for linear distance encoders. Our ensemble employs a random matching circuit architecture consisting of $T$ independent permutation-invariant layers. In each layer, the qubits are paired up by a uniformly random perfect matching, and a random independent two-qubit Clifford gate is applied to each pair. The gate distribution need not be uniform over, or even have full support on, the two-qubit Clifford group; rather, we allow for very general distributions on Clifford gates satisfying three regularity conditions. In particular, the construction can be implemented using $n/2$ CNOT gates on randomly matched pairs in each layer, with parallel one-qubit Clifford twirls. These regularity conditions allow us to reduce the second-moment dynamics of our random circuits to a reversible Markov chain on binary support strings. We establish logarithmic hitting-time bounds for this Markov chain and comparisons of its stationary distribution to prove the coding properties of the circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the usability crisis in quantum software by conducting a stakeholder analysis to define user personas. By identifying diverse needs—ranging from business users to algorithm developers—it proposes a framework for designing software that correctly balances high-level abstraction with necessary hardware details. This is crucial for advancing the practical application of quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not focused on physical dynamics, the paper addresses the crucial engineering and usability challenges of quantum computing, which dictates how theoretical models (like Hamiltonians) can be practically implemented and utilized by different scientific communities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is designing well-structured quantum software due to the rapid advancement of quantum hardware and the inherent complexity of quantum computing.

**Main result.** The authors derived eleven distinct user and stakeholder personas, providing a common language to guide the creation of user-tailored quantum software.

**Method.** The study employed a stakeholder-based analysis, combining insights from an expert focus group and qualitative interviews with quantum computing practitioners.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the software layer interacting with quantum hardware, spanning from abstract quantum circuits and Hamiltonians to specific physical qubit architectures (short, mid, and long-term regimes).

**Key observables.** The required level of abstraction, the functional goals of the user (e.g., scientific insight vs. business motive), and the necessary hardware details.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Technological maturity stages (short-term, mid-term, long-term hardware capabilities) and the balance between abstraction level and hardware specificity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derived personas are considered a 'persona-hypothesis' intended as a basis for future product development, and their applicability is limited to similar contexts.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Goal-Directed Design process phases. Figure 4 maps the identified personas across different layers of quantum software abstraction.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the methodology (focus groups/interviews), presents the resulting eleven personas categorized by use case and required abstraction level, and discusses the implications for software design.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The advancement of quantum hardware and the intricacies of quantum computing make well-designed quantum software increasingly necessary. Due to the interdisciplinarity of the field, it is crucial to understand the perspectives and specific needs of involved stakeholders, for example, to balance the desired level of abstraction with the exposition of (hardware)-specific details. In this work, we conduct a stakeholder-based analysis to identify personas of quantum software as a means of creating meaningful, user-tailored quantum software. We conducted an expert focus group at a Dagstuhl seminar in 2024 and qualitative interviews with practitioners at conference IEEE QCE in 2025, from which we derive eleven personas of potential users and stakeholders for quantum software. We discuss these personas regarding their use cases, interests, constraints and abstraction level.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Layer-Restricted Boltzmann Machine (LRBM) as a constructive framework for modeling emergent systems, inspired by statistical phylogenetics. By inferring and enforcing a unidirectional flow across hierarchical entropic scales, the LRBM successfully models structure in images, language, and critically, in enzyme sequences. The success in designing functional enzymes that retain fold but require all hierarchical layers for full function suggests a general, learnable statistical logic for complex system construction.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using hierarchical entropic constraints derived from statistical physics principles (like those used in analyzing disordered systems) to guide the construction of complex, functional biological architectures (like enzymes) suggests a universal organizing principle applicable beyond standard ML tasks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the lack of constructive logic in current AI models for building complex emergent systems, which typically arise through multi-scale interactions.

**Main result.** The authors propose the Layer-Restricted Boltzmann Machine (LRBM), demonstrating that inferring and enforcing a unidirectional flow across hierarchical entropic scales provides a general, data-driven method for constructing emergent systems.

**Method.** The methodology involves inferring discrete scales of entropy from an ensemble of existing data (e.g., natural enzymes) and constraining information flow sequentially across these scales to build the LRBM architecture.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Layer-Restricted Boltzmann Machine (LRBM), which is applied to diverse systems including digit images, natural language, and the chorismate mutase (CM) enzyme sequence space. The system exhibits a clear hierarchy where lower layers capture global structure and higher layers refine fine-grained features.

**Key observables.** Enzyme fold emergence, functional activity (bimodal activity), reconstruction fidelity from noise, and the block-structured parameter matrix of the LRBM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of discrete entropic scales (K), the coupling strength between adjacent layers, and the sequence divergence percentage for synthetic enzymes (up to 53%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the underlying generative process of an emergent system can be characterized solely by a hierarchical structure of entropic constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MNIST reconstruction process showing layer-by-layer refinement from global structure to local detail, and the LRBM structure derived from simulated and natural ensembles.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for constructive models, proposes the LRBM based on statistical phylogenetics, validates the model on MNIST images, and culminates in a stringent test using the *in vivo* functional assay of synthetic chorismate mutase enzymes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Emergent systems - systems containing multi-scale interactions - often arise through iteration rather than explicit forward design. As such, design principles for building emergent systems have been under-explored. Current artificial intelligence architectures, while useful for generation, do not provide a logic for how to construct emergent systems. Inspired by statistical phylogenetics, we show that inferring scales of system entropy then explicitly constraining information flow from low to high entropic scales yields data-driven, stepwise 'constructive models' of emergent systems. We term the resulting architecture the Layer-Restricted Boltzmann Machine (LRBM). Applied identically to digit images, natural language, and enzyme sequences, LRBM models follow hierarchical logic: lower layers encode global structure from which higher layers encode fine-grained features. As a stringent experimental challenge for LRBM-based construction, we built and evaluated 160 constructive trajectories of synthetic and 1,130 natural chorismate mutase (CM) enzymes in an in vivo functional assay. Designed CMs functioned up to 53% sequence divergent from the nearest natural homolog. Notably, enzyme fold emerged at intermediate LRBM layers while function arose only upon satisfying all layers. Fold was therefore necessary but insufficient for function in this enzyme family. Our results suggest a shared hierarchical, learnable statistical logic for constructing emergent systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the successful implementation of the machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation functional into the CP2K simulation code using the GauXC library. By creating a validated interface, the authors enable high-accuracy DFT calculations for molecular systems using ML potentials. This advances the field by bridging complex ML models with established quantum chemistry codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant advancement in computational materials science by integrating advanced machine learning potentials into established, production-level quantum simulation codes, improving accuracy while maintaining computational feasibility.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need to improve the accuracy of Kohn-Sham Density-Functional Theory (DFT) beyond traditional approximations without resorting to computationally expensive explicitly correlated methods.

**Main result.** The authors successfully implemented the machine-learned Skala-1.1 exchange-correlation functional into the CP2K code via the GauXC library, achieving an aggregate mean absolute deviation on the dietGMTKN55 benchmark within 0.020 kcal/mol of the reference value.

**Method.** The core method involves creating a robust interface (GauXC) that maps the learned functional's requirements onto CP2K's density representation, allowing the evaluation of XC energy, potential matrix, and derivatives.

**Model / system.** The work is applied to molecular electronic structure calculations, using benchmark suites like dietGMTKN55 and testing on water-hexamer clusters, covering both all-electron and valence-only density representations.

**Key observables.** XC energy ($E_{	ext{Skala}}^{	ext{xc}}$), AO XC potential matrix ($V_{	ext{Skala}}^{\mu
u, \sigma}$), nuclear derivatives (forces), and Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) on benchmark sets.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The benchmark accuracy is quantified by the MAD of $1.255 	ext{ kcal mol}^{-1}$ vs. the reference $1.235 	ext{ kcal mol}^{-1}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interface design isolates implementation errors from functional differences, and the primary focus is on the AO density matrix representation, excluding auxiliary Plane-Wave grid details.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the division of labor between CP2K, GauXC, and Skala, and show representative derivative checks for the molecular GauXC interface.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the formulation of the Skala-1.1 interface in CP2K via GauXC, validates the implementation by comparing against native PBE and finite-difference checks, and reports benchmark results on the dietGMTKN55 suite.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Machine-learned exchange--correlation (XC) functionals offer a route to improve Kohn--Sham density-functional theory without incurring the cost of explicitly correlated electronic-structure methods. Their use in production simulation codes, however, requires a well-defined mapping between the learned model and the host-code density representation. We formulate and implement a Skala-1.1 interface in CP2K through the external GauXC library. CP2K supplies the geometry, Gaussian basis, spin-resolved atomic-orbital density matrix, and communicator, while GauXC evaluates the XC energy, atomic-orbital potential matrix, and available nuclear derivatives. The interface accepts both all-electron and valence-only density matrices. The latter may arise from separable dual-space pseudopotentials or molecular effective-core potentials. Implementation errors are isolated from functional differences by comparing the Perdew--Burke--Ernzerhof (PBE) functional evaluated through GauXC with native CP2K PBE. The resulting interface gives consistent energies, forces validated against finite-difference total-energy checks, and force-based molecular-virial diagnostics for representative molecular cases. The dietGMTKN55 benchmark suite is evaluated with an all-electron Gaussian augmented plane-wave treatment for elements up to bromine and def2 effective-core potentials for the heavier elements. The resulting aggregate mean absolute deviation of 1.255 kcal/mol is within 0.020 kcal/mol of the corresponding Skala reference value of 1.235 kcal/mol. This work establishes a validated molecular implementation of Skala in CP2K through GauXC.

</details>

### [Multicast quantum network coding as optimal symmetric universal cloning over a quantum network](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18471v1)

**Authors:** Go Kato, Mio Murao, Masaki Owari  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18471v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Fig. 1. Undirected butterﬂy network (left) and directed butterﬂy network (right): These networks have one source node s1, two terminal nodes t1 and t2, and three intermediate nodes v1, v2, and v3.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quantum network coding protocol to achieve the perfect multicast of symmetric universal clones of unknown quantum states. By extending existing network coding techniques, the authors establish necessary and sufficient conditions based on graph connectivity (minimum cut) for this quantum information task. This work advances the theory of quantum communication by providing a robust method for state distribution in noisy or complex quantum network topologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, information-theoretic framework for distributing quantum information (clones) across a network, which is fundamental to quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of perfectly multicasting symmetric universal clones of unknown quantum states across a quantum network.

**Main result.** It constructs a protocol showing that perfect multicast is achievable under specific graph connectivity conditions, extending previous quantum network coding results.

**Method.** The authors extend the quantum network coding protocol by leveraging the structure of the underlying graph and utilizing concepts from classical network coding theory.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum network modeled by an undirected graph G, where edges represent noiseless q-dimensional quantum channels. Free classical communication is assumed to be available.

**Key observables.** The achievable state on the terminal nodes, which is shown to be proportional to the initial input state tensor product with the cloning factor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the input state (d_l), the size of the quantum channel alphabet (q, a prime power), and the minimum cut capacity of the derived directed graph G'.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the availability of free classical communication and relies on the existence of an acyclic directed graph G' derived from G that satisfies certain minimum cut conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the structure of the quantum network using an undirected and directed butterfly network example, showing source and terminal nodes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon classical network coding theory, extends it to the quantum domain for multi-source multicast, and provides a detailed proof confirming the solvability of the quantum multicast network coding problem for cloning.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the problem of perfectly multicasting symmetric universal clones of unknown quantum states over quantum networks with free classical communication.   We construct a protocol that multicasts symmetric universal clones of input states from multiple source nodes by extending the quantum network coding protocol proposed by Kobayashi et al. We further establish a sufficient condition for perfect multicast in the single-source setting. Specifically, we show that when a single copy of a $q^r$-dimensional input state is available at the source node, where $q$ is a sufficiently large prime power, perfect multicast of the corresponding symmetric universal clone is achievable using a small amount of entanglement shared among the target nodes.   This result holds for quantum networks represented by an undirected graph $G$, where each edge corresponds to a noiseless $q$-dimensional quantum channel, provided that there exists an acyclic directed graph $G'$ obtained by assigning directions to the edges of $G$ such that the minimum cut of $G'$ is at least $r$.

</details>

**Authors:** Yvkai Zhao, Lin Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18421v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: The three color layers of the example and the merged edge-colored complete multigraph Γ(M) on V = {1, . . . , 8}. The three layers are drawn in blue (solid), orange (dashed), and green (dotted), respectively.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.18421_figures/2608.18421_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 2: Flowchart of the two-phase equivalence-testing algorithm. Phase 1 checks necessary invariants, and Phase 2 performs the exact layered backtracking search.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the classification of complete orthogonal product bases (OPBs) for $n$-qubit systems by mapping the problem onto graph isomorphism. The authors prove that equivalence is determined by the isomorphism of an associated edge-colored multigraph. They derive bounds showing the number of such bases grows doubly exponentially and present a complex, two-phase algorithm to test for equivalence.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical characterization of quantum state bases and the development of an algorithm to test equivalence are fundamental theoretical tools applicable to understanding quantum resource theories and quantum circuit complexity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to classify all complete orthogonal product bases (OPBs) for an $n$-qubit system up to natural equivalence.

**Main result.** The classification problem is reduced to determining the isomorphism of associated edge-colored complete multigraphs, and the number of equivalence classes $a_n$ exhibits doubly exponential growth, $a_n = 2^{2^{n+o(n)}}$.

**Method.** The authors utilize a formal-matrix formalism, mapping the OPB matrix to a multigraph, and developing a two-phase algorithm combining invariant checking with a layered isomorphism search to test equivalence.

**Model / system.** The system is an $n$-qubit quantum system, mathematically modeled by an OPB formal matrix $M \in O(n)$. This matrix is associated with an edge-colored complete multigraph $\Gamma(M)$ on $2n$ vertices.

**Key observables.** The spectrum $\pi(M)$ (part sizes $d_\alpha$), the multiset of column spectra $\{\pi_j(M)\}$, and the multiset of edge multiplicities $\{\mu(r, s)\}$ are key invariants used for comparison.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits $n$, the number of variables $v$ (bounded by $2n-1$), and the size of the vertex set $N=2n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The equivalence of two OPB matrices is rigorously proven to be equivalent to the isomorphism of their associated multigraphs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the multigraph construction for a 3-qubit OPB, and Figure 2 provides a flowchart summarizing the two-phase equivalence-testing algorithm.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the formal-matrix formalism, proves the equivalence theorem linking OPB equivalence to graph isomorphism, derives bounds and asymptotics for the number of classes $a_n$, and concludes with the description and complexity analysis of the two-phase equivalence testing algorithm.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study complete orthogonal product bases (OPBs) of an $n$-qubit system via the formal-matrix formalism for multiqubit OPBs. To each OPB formal matrix we associate an edge-colored complete multigraph, and we prove that two formal matrices are equivalent if and only if their multigraphs are isomorphic, thereby reducing the classification of OPBs to graph isomorphism. Via subcube partitions and a lemma of Tarsi, we upper bound the number of variables by $2^n - 1$. We also study lower and upper bounds on the number $a_n$ of equivalence classes of $n$-qubit OPBs, by showing that $\binom{a_{n-1}+1}{2} \le a_n \le B_{2^{n-1}}^n$, where $B_m$ denotes the number of partitions of an $m$-element set. These bounds yield the asymptotic behavior $a_n = 2^{2^{n+o(n)}}$. Finally, we obtain a two-phase algorithm that decides whether two OPB formal matrices are equivalent, together with its correctness proof and complexity analysis. The complexity is exponential in the worst case.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a specific total Boolean function, Cheat-Shapen, to demonstrate a separation between quantum and classical communication complexity. It achieves a polylogarithmic quantum communication protocol while requiring a super-polynomial randomized protocol. This highlights the computational power of quantum communication protocols for certain tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, information-theoretic example of quantum advantage in communication complexity, which is a fundamental area of quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to construct a total Boolean function that demonstrates a significant separation between its quantum communication complexity and its classical randomized communication complexity.

**Main result.** The authors present a function, Cheat-Shapen, with a polylogarithmic two-message quantum communication protocol, while its randomized communication complexity is shown to be super-polynomial in the input size.

**Method.** The construction combines three elements: the shifted approximate equality problem (Shape), a communication cheat-sheet construction, and a fully linear PCP derived from multiplication gates.

**Model / system.** The analysis is based on the communication complexity of a specific total Boolean function, Cheat-Shapen, which is defined using multiple instances of the Shape problem.

**Key observables.** Quantum communication complexity ($	ext{Q}_{1/3}(	ext{Cheat-Shapen}) \in O(\log^3 n \log \log n)$) and randomized communication complexity ($	ext{R}_{1/3}(	ext{Cheat-Shapen}) \in \Omega(rac{N_n}{6} \sqrt{n} \log^{7/3} N_n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The input size parameters $n$ and $k$, and the total input size $N_n = 2nk + M(1 + \ell_n)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum protocol relies on an established one-way consequence for the Shape problem, and the PCP construction requires viewing $1 - D_{n,k}$ as an arithmetic circuit over a characteristic-two field.

**Paper structure.** The paper proves Theorem 1 by combining the derived quantum protocol bound (Equation 12) with the established classical lower bound (Equations 15 and 17).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a total function with a polylogarithmic two-message quantum protocol, whereas every randomised protocol, even with arbitrarily many rounds, requires polynomial communication.

</details>
