**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel reinforcement learning framework that enhances quantum circuit optimization by integrating a deterministic Commutation-and-Reduction (CR) algorithm. By offloading elementary error corrections to the CR routine, the RL agent focuses its learning capacity on complex structural improvements. The resulting RL+CR method achieves state-of-the-art circuit compression, outperforming existing methods on simulated quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical compilation and optimization bottleneck in quantum computing, providing a data-driven tool to make quantum algorithms more robust for near-term hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is optimizing quantum circuits to reduce gate count and depth to mitigate errors caused by decoherence on NISQ devices.

**Main result.** The proposed RL+CR framework significantly outperforms standard RL agents and even state-of-the-art transpilers, achieving substantial gate and depth reductions across multiple gate sets.

**Method.** A reinforcement learning agent is trained within an environment that deterministically applies a Commutation-and-Reduction (CR) algorithm after every action, allowing the agent to focus on non-trivial optimizations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by quantum circuits implemented on near-term quantum hardware, tested across two gate sets: the universal Clifford+T basis and the CNOT+Pauli basis.

**Key observables.** Gate count reduction percentage, circuit depth reduction percentage, and the final optimized circuit structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization is evaluated across varying qubit numbers (e.g., up to 20 qubits) and involves hyperparameters like learning rates and discount factors for the PPO algorithm.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that deterministic rules (handled by CR) can be separated from the complex, non-trivial optimizations that require machine learning.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the RL framework loop, the two-phase CR execution, and comparative learning curves showing RL+CR's superior performance over standard RL and CR baselines across different qubit counts.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the RL+CR methodology (including the CR algorithm and the PPO formulation), presents rigorous comparisons against baselines (including Qiskit), and concludes with performance metrics across various circuit sizes and bases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The practical implementation of quantum algorithms on noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices encounters operational limitations due to decoherence and other sources of noise inherent in real hardware. To mitigate these errors while preserving the original functionality of the algorithm, shorter quantum circuits are therefore preferred. This motivates the development of effective quantum circuit optimization algorithms. Learning-based approaches have emerged as a leading candidate, yet existing autonomous agents remain inefficient, spending most of their training capacity rediscovering elementary reductions that deterministic rule-based methods already handle reliably. To address this challenge, we propose a reinforcement learning framework that embeds a deterministic Commutation-and-Reduction (CR) algorithm directly into the training environment. After every agent action, the CR algorithm automatically resolves elementary commutations and cancellations, enabling the agent to focus its learning capacity on the non-trivial optimizations where reinforcement learning adds real value. Empirical evaluation across two gate sets, the universal Clifford+T basis and the CNOT+Pauli basis, shows that RL+CR produces shorter circuits than a standard RL agent at all tested scales. We demonstrate that RL trained on smaller quantum circuits can be applied to larger quantum circuits. On 20-qubit Clifford+T circuits, five times larger than the training circuits, RL+CR removes twice as many gates as standard RL. This work provides a robust approach that could accelerate the compilation and optimization processes for future fault-tolerant and utility-scale quantum systems.

</details>

### [Quantum Speedups Require Structure or Depth](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19158v1)

**Authors:** Guy Blanc, Jordan Docter, Carmen Strassle, Li-Yang Tan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.19158v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2608.19158_figures/2608.19158_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 3: A classical query algorithm for approximating a quantum query algorithm.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the fundamental limits of quantum speedup by examining the simulation conjecture. It proves that for unstructured problems, achieving superpolynomial speedups requires quantum circuits of significant depth, suggesting that most known quantum advantages stem from exploiting problem structure. The work uses query weight analysis to establish these bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on complexity theory, the techniques developed for bounding quantum query complexity and relating it to circuit depth are fundamental tools applicable to understanding resource limitations in quantum simulation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which quantum algorithms can achieve superpolynomial speedups over classical computation, formalized by the simulation conjecture.

**Main result.** The authors show that for parallel quantum algorithms, the simulation conjecture fails for unstructured problems unless the quantum circuits possess superconstant depth, contrasting with known speedups for structured problems.

**Method.** The analysis employs query weight analysis (a 'whitebox' approach) to derive lower bounds on the query complexity, contrasting it with influence-based methods.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum query algorithms, analyzing the query weight allocation ($W_i(x)$) of a $t$-query, $d$-round quantum algorithm $A$ for a function $F: \{0, 1\}^N 	o \{0, 1, *\}$.

**Key observables.** Query weights ($W_i(x)$), acceptance probability ($f(x)$), and the $\delta$ parameter quantifying the separation of acceptance probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Query depth ($d$), parallel queries per round ($t$), and the separation parameter $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results heavily rely on the strong version of Conjecture 1, which relates query weights to the simulation conjecture, and the Aaronson–Ambainis conjecture.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the structure of a $t$-query $d$-round quantum algorithm, which is central to the complexity analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the simulation conjecture, developing the query weight framework (Conjecture 1), proving simulation bounds for parallel algorithms (Theorem 1), and establishing equivalences between random-oracle and unrelativized complexity separations (Theorems 2 and 3).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

One of the most basic conjectures in quantum complexity theory states that every $t$-query quantum algorithm can be simulated on most inputs by a $\mathrm{poly}(t)$-query classical algorithm. If true, this would provide broad justification for the need for structure in quantum speedups.   We settle this conjecture for parallel quantum algorithms, showing that every $t$-query $d$-round quantum algorithm can be simulated on most inputs with $t^{O(d^2)}$ classical queries. This suggests that for unstructured problems, superpolynomial speedups would require quantum circuits of superconstant depth, and exponential speedups would further require polynomial depth. In contrast, most known speedups for structured problems are achieved by highly parallel, low-depth algorithms.   Our techniques also carry new implications for the status of $\mathsf{BPP}$ vs. $\mathsf{BQP}$ relative to a random oracle, a similarly longstanding problem.

</details>

### [Realizing Logical Diagonal Gates via Transversal Physical $Z$-Rotations in CSS Codes](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19094v1)

**Authors:** K. Sai Mineesh Reddy, Navin Kashyap  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.19094v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper characterizes CSS quantum error-correcting codes that can implement logical diagonal gates using transversal physical Z-rotations. It introduces a powerful 'appending construction' that allows researchers to systematically extend any given code to support an arbitrary number of desired logical Z-rotations. This advances the theory of fault-tolerant quantum computation by providing a scalable method for code design.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, constructive framework for building quantum codes capable of implementing complex, non-Clifford logical gates required for universal quantum computation, which is a core theoretical challenge in quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of designing quantum error-correcting codes (CSS codes) that can fault-tolerantly implement a diverse set of logical diagonal gates using only transversal physical Z-rotations.

**Main result.** The authors develop an 'appending construction' that allows extending any existing CSS code to support multiple desired logical Z-rotations, although this increases the physical qubit overhead.

**Method.** The methodology involves characterizing the necessary conditions for transversal physical Z-rotations to realize specific logical gates, leading to the development of the systematic appending construction.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the quantum error-correcting code, specifically Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes defined by nested classical codes $C_2 \subseteq C_1$. The operations are physical transversal Z-rotations.

**Key observables.** Code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$, the realizable logical diagonal gates (e.g., single-qubit or multi-controlled-Z rotations), and the associated physical transversal rotation parameters $(p, w)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of appended qubits ($n''$), the number of target logical gates, and the required fidelity/distance of the resulting code.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to gates realizable via transversal physical Z-rotations, which limits the achievable logical gates to specific diagonal forms.

**Paper structure.** The paper first characterizes which CSS codes can realize target logical gates via transversal Z-rotations, establishing limitations. It then introduces and details the 'appending construction' to systematically extend codes to support multiple gates, followed by detailed mathematical proofs concerning the necessary and sufficient conditions for gate realization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes, constructed from nested classical codes $C_2 \subseteq C_1$, are typically optimized for good code parameters. However, practical quantum computing equally demands fault-tolerant logical gates. In this work, we characterize nested pairs $(C_1, C_2)$ whose resulting CSS codes realize a target logical diagonal gate via transversal physical $Z$-rotations. In doing so, we recover a result of Camps-Moreno et al. that CSS codes can realize only logical single-qubit $Z$-rotations and multi-qubit controlled-$Z$ rotations via transversal physical $Z$-rotations. Building on our characterization, we develop the ''appending construction'', that takes as input an $[[n',k']]$ CSS code $Q'$ and a target logical $Z$-rotation (single-qubit or multi-controlled) $U_L$, and extends $Q'$ by systematically appending $n''$ physical qubits to obtain an $[[n,k]]$ CSS code $Q$ with $n = n'+n''$ and $k=k'$. The target logical gate $U_L$ is realized in $Q$ by applying a well-chosen physical transversal $Z$-rotation to the $n''$ appended physical qubits. The CSS code $Q$ may incur a loss in minimum distance, but the loss can be controlled through the parameter choices made in the construction. By repeatedly applying the appending construction, we can extend any CSS code $Q'$ to obtain a CSS code $Q$ that supports fault-tolerant implementations of multiple desired logical $Z$-rotations. The cost to be paid for this is the increased physical qubit overhead as the number of target logical gates grows.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that applying uniaxial strain to Ge/SiGe quantum wells can dramatically enhance the Rashba spin-orbit coupling in 2D hole gases. This enhancement boosts the Rabi frequency of hole spin qubits to record-breaking speeds (40 GHz) while simultaneously creating an operational regime immune to electric noise, paving the way for faster, higher-fidelity solid-state quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, material-science-based pathway (strain engineering) to enhance fundamental quantum coupling parameters (SOC) in solid-state systems, directly impacting the feasibility and speed of solid-state qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the inherently weak Rashba spin-orbit coupling (SOC) in 2D hole gases, which limits the speed and fidelity of electric-field-controlled spin manipulation for quantum computing applications.

**Main result.** The application of uniaxial strain, accessible via CMOS technology, can substantially enhance the linear Rashba SOC in Ge/SiGe quantum wells, boosting the hole spin qubit Rabi frequency to an unprecedented 40 GHz.

**Method.** The study employs atomistic semi-empirical pseudopotential method (SEPM) calculations combined with model Hamiltonian analysis to theoretically predict the effect of strain on the SOC parameters.

**Model / system.** The system involves hole spin qubits confined in Ge/SiGe quantum wells (QWs). The physics is governed by the effective SOC Hamiltonian, which is analyzed for its dependence on applied uniaxial and biaxial strain.

**Key observables.** Rabi frequency ($\omega_R$), linear Rashba SOC strength, single-qubit gate fidelity, and spin precession length.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial strain ($\epsilon_u$), biaxial strain ($\epsilon_b$), and the vertical electric field ($E_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement mechanism is attributed to strain-induced heavy-hole-light-hole (HH-LH) mixing, which is otherwise symmetry-forbidden.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare qubit performance across platforms, illustrate the strain engineering approach by showing energy dispersion and SOC parameters ($\alpha_1, \alpha_2$) as functions of strain and electric field.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of weak SOC, then details how strain engineering enhances the SOC parameters ($\alpha_1, \alpha_2$) using SEPM calculations. Finally, it demonstrates the resulting superfast qubit operation regime, including immunity to electric noise.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional (2D) electron/hole gases confined in semiconductor heterostructures suffer from weak Rashba spin-orbit coupling (SOC) for manipulating spin degreee of freedom via an electric rather than a magnetic field. Here, we show that complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor technology-accessible strain could substantially enhance the linear Rashba SOC of the top hole subband in Ge/SiGe quantum wells (QWs) to a level comparable to that of 2D Rashba materials through enhancing the mixture of the light-hole and heavy-hole bands. We further show that strongly enhanced Rashba SOC boosts the Rabi frequency of hole spin qubits confined in Ge/SiGe QWs by two orders of magnitude to an unprecedented 40 GHz, more than one order of magnitude faster than other qubit platforms. We also demonstrate that the hole spin rotation with Rabi frequency > 25 GHz enters a new regime being immune to gate control-induced electric noise, opening a new avenue to simultaneously improve the gate speed and gate fidelity. Our findings provide a new routine to substantially enhance the Rashba SOC in 2D semiconductor hole gases to a level that is great for spintronic applications.

</details>

**Authors:** Damian Rovara, Robert Wille  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.18206v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper introduces a block-based programming framework, built on Scratch, designed to teach quantum design automation. It solves the difficulty novices face when learning complex concepts like circuit compilation and resource estimation by providing an intuitive, visual environment. The framework allows users to build circuits and apply advanced automation techniques, significantly lowering the barrier to entry for quantum computing education.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on pedagogy, the work directly addresses the software engineering challenges of quantum computing, which is crucial for any theoretical physicist needing to translate abstract quantum models into executable, verifiable code.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** As quantum circuits become complex for physical execution, design automation—including compilation, resource estimation, and verification—is necessary but presents a steep learning curve for novices.

**Main result.** The authors developed and tested a block-based programming framework, extending Scratch, which successfully lowers the entry barrier to understanding and applying quantum design automation concepts.

**Method.** They implemented a block-based programming environment that allows users to build quantum circuits and embed them within classical control logic, enabling the direct application and comparison of various design automation techniques.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum circuits and their execution flow, allowing for the integration of quantum operations with classical control structures like loops and conditionals.

**Key observables.** Circuit correctness (verification), resource requirements, and execution cost.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity of the quantum circuit, the level of abstraction required for the user (novice vs. expert).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that adopting a proven, accessible pedagogical methodology from classical computer science (Scratch) is the most effective way to teach advanced quantum design automation concepts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the implementation of Grover's algorithm, contrasting the limitations of standard visual editors (requiring unrolling loops) with the proposed Scratch extension's ability to handle loops and control flow naturally.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the need for design automation, critiques existing tools (visual editors and textual representations) for their limitations in handling complexity, proposes the Scratch-based block framework, and validates its efficacy through a user study.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum circuits grow beyond small toy examples, preparing them for execution on physical devices becomes increasingly complex. Design automation is therefore essential for scalable quantum computing: Compilation procedures optimize resource requirements and transform circuits to a format compatible with specific hardware; resource estimation evaluates execution cost; verification methods prove circuit correctness. However, these concepts present a steep learning curve for novices, particularly when quantum circuits are introduced through low-level textual representations. To address this, we present a block-based programming framework for quantum design automation, implemented as an extension to the Scratch programming platform. This system allows users to build quantum circuits as a sequence of blocks and embed them in classical control logic to perform evaluations, compare simulation results, and directly apply different design automation techniques. We evaluated the approach in a user study with computer science students, who completed guided exercises using the platform and provided structured feedback in the form of self-reports and short knowledge assessments. Results demonstrate strong understanding and confidence in quantum design automation concepts, suggesting that the block-based approach successfully lowers the entry barrier to quantum design automation. The implemented framework is open-source and available at https://github.com/munich-quantum-toolkit/scratch-quantum.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates enhancing the sensitivity of SAW resonators by engineering their spectral response using Fano interference. The authors demonstrate the simultaneous control of both electromagnetic and acoustic Fano interference by modifying the resonator's acoustic confinement. This technique proves that spectral asymmetry can be tuned, offering a promising route for advanced classical and quantum sensing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer spectral features via controlled interference mechanisms (Fano resonance) provides a powerful, non-traditional method for enhancing sensor sensitivity, which is relevant for developing high-precision quantum transducers or detectors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the sensitivity of Surface Acoustic Wave (SAW) resonators for sensing applications by engineering their spectral response.

**Main result.** The work successfully demonstrates the simultaneous introduction of both electromagnetic and acoustic Fano interference to shape the spectral response, confirming that Bragg mirror design acts as a tunable knob for spectral symmetry.

**Method.** The researchers measured the transmission spectrum ($S_{21}$) of SAW resonators across varying temperatures and systematically modified the acoustic confinement using different Bragg mirror configurations.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a SAW resonator fabricated on Y-cut LiNbO3, operating at GHz frequencies. The analysis models the spectral response using the Fano lineshape function, which accounts for interference between resonant and continuum scattering pathways.

**Key observables.** Spectral line shape asymmetry, quantified by the Fano factor ($q$), resonance frequency ($f_0$), and the temperature dependence of these parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (ambient to $\sim 1 	ext{ K}$), acoustic reflectivity (controlled by Bragg mirror pairs), and the Fano parameter $q$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interference effects can be separated by systematically modifying the acoustic confinement, allowing independent analysis of electromagnetic and acoustic contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show schematics of the interference setup, measured transmission spectra ($S_{21}$) exhibiting asymmetric dips, and plots detailing the temperature dependence of the Fano factor $|q(T)|$ and the resonance frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of using Fano interference to enhance SAW sensor sensitivity. It details the experimental setup using multiple devices with varying acoustic confinement. The core of the work involves fitting the measured spectral data to the Fano lineshape function to quantify the interplay between acoustic and electromagnetic interference, concluding with temperature-dependent analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Surface acoustic wave-based resonators are sensitive probes of condensed matter systems, as well as surface-selective sensors for chemistry and biology. Surface acoustic wave devices have also been integrated into hybrid quantum systems with qubit platforms for applications in quantum information processing and sensing. The sensitivity of piezoelectric surface wave-based resonators to investigate these various systems can be enhanced by optimizing the device architecture and electrical measurement techniques. Alternatively, tailoring the spectral symmetry, arising from interference effects, offers a promising route to further improve sensitivity. In this work, we demonstrate the simultaneous introduction of both electromagnetic and acoustic Fano interference to shape the spectral response of GHz-frequency surface acoustic wave resonators. By systematically modifying the acoustic reflectivity of the resonators, we are able to isolate and analyze each interference mechanism independently. The broad range of temperature operation, from ambient to cryogenic temperatures highlights the potential for both classical and quantum sensing applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using patterned light fields as a tunable alternative to physical strain or twisting to create superlattices in bilayer graphene. By controlling the electromagnetic drive, they can independently modulate the electronic couplings, leading to the emergence of flat bands and measurable topological invariants like the Chern number. This establishes a highly flexible platform for realizing and studying moiré-like quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering of topological matter using external, tunable fields, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics approaches to condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To find a flexible and tunable method to engineer flat bands and topological phases in bilayer graphene, bypassing the limitations of physically fixed strain or twisting.

**Main result.** Patterned electromagnetic fields allow for the independent and tunable control of intralayer and interlayer couplings, inducing isolated, nearly flat bands and rich topological structures characterized by calculable Chern numbers.

**Method.** The study employs Floquet engineering by driving the system with patterned light fields, analyzing the resulting quasienergy band structure and topological invariants.

**Model / system.** The system is untwisted bilayer graphene (AA- and AB-stacked) subjected to time-periodic driving from patterned in-plane and out-of-plane electromagnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Electronic band structure (minibands), quasienergy band structures, and valley Chern numbers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency ($\Omega$), superlattice period ($L$), and the relative strengths of the patterned fields ($a_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the Floquet-Bloch formalism and assumes the driving frequency is high enough to prevent overlap of Floquet replicas.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the band structures (quasienergy plots) under various driving conditions (circularly polarized, longitudinal, combined) as a function of driving frequency and superlattice period, alongside phase diagrams showing Chern numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the problem, details the optical coupling mechanisms (intralayer vs. interlayer), and then presents results comparing the topological outcomes (Chern numbers) and band flattening achieved using different combinations of patterned light drives.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional superlattices in van der Waals materials host flat bands and nontrivial topology, most famously at the so-called magic angles of twisted bilayer graphene, where flat bands give rise to correlated and topological phases. Yet these superlattices are usually created by twisting the layers or applying strain, and once a sample is fabricated, their period is fixed and extremely difficult to tune. Here we propose an alternative route: imprint the superlattice optically, using patterned electromagnetic fields rather than a physical twist or strain. We show that patterned in-plane cir- cularly polarized light and a combined drive consisting of a patterned out-of-plane longitudinal field and a uniform circularly polarized field produce isolated bands in both AA- and AB-stacked bilayer graphene. In AB stacking, the central bands additionally become nearly flat, capturing key features of a driven moir/'e superlattice. In this approach, the superlattice period is set by the illumination and is straightforward to tune, and circularly polarized light breaks time-reversal symmetry. Computing the valley Chern numbers of the central bands, we find a rich topological structure with several phase transitions in both stackings. Our results establish light-induced superlattices as a flexible and tunable platform for engineering flat bands and topological phases in bilayer graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study demonstrates Floquet engineering of topological bands in TMD heterobilayers using near-infrared to visible light. By applying the Floquet formalism, the authors predict that periodic driving can induce topological phase transitions, leading to high Chern numbers and robust chiral edge states. This establishes TMDs as a promising platform for realizing engineered topological quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies concepts from quantum optics and open quantum systems (periodic driving) to solid-state physics, demonstrating how external fields can be used to engineer fundamental quantum properties like topology in solid-state materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how periodic driving using light can induce topological phase transitions and engineer topological insulating bands in semiconductor van der Waals heterobilayers.

**Main result.** The system exhibits non-trivial Floquet topological insulating phases, achieving Chern numbers up to $\pm 2$ and demonstrating chiral edge states indicative of robust topological protection.

**Method.** The Floquet formalism is applied to a light-coupled $\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{p}$ Hamiltonian, leading to the calculation of topological invariants like the Chern number.

**Model / system.** The physical system is based on commensurate type-II TMD heterobilayers (e.g., $	ext{WSe}_2/	ext{MoSe}_2$). The model uses an effective Hamiltonian derived from the lowest-order $\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{p}$ theory, incorporating the interaction with an incident electromagnetic wave.

**Key observables.** Topological phase diagrams (Chern numbers $C$), topological band gaps ($\Delta_T$), and chiral edge states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Light frequency ($\Omega$), electric-field amplitude ($E_0$), and polarization (LPL, CPL, RCPL). The analysis focuses on the near-resonant regime ($\hbar\Omega \gtrsim \Delta_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes a perfectly aligned, commensurate heterobilayer, and the analysis relies on truncating the Floquet Hamiltonian to a finite set of harmonics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the topological phase diagrams showing regions of non-trivial topology, band structures confirming the gap opening, and LDOS calculations visualizing the predicted chiral edge states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the effective Hamiltonian using the Floquet formalism, calculates the Chern number as a function of driving parameters, and then analyzes the resulting topological phase diagrams and edge state properties for different stacking and polarization configurations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that periodic driving with near-infrared to visible light drives topological phase transitions in the photon-dressed band structure of transition-metal dichalcogenide heterobilayers. We apply the Floquet formalism to a light-coupled lowest-order $\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{p}$ Hamiltonian, and obtain an effective four-band model that correctly captures the essential photon-dressed bands of type-II TMD heterobilayers in the vicinity of the first photon resonance. Crossings between the zero- and one-photon sectors effectively invert the bands, yielding topological phases with Chern numbers up to $\pm 2$ and gaps of order 10 meV. Our results establish TMDs as prime candidates for engineering bands with higher Chern numbers, and exploring driven topological states in solid state media.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an efficient, dimension-agnostic method to calculate real-space lattice Green's functions for doped tight-binding models. By leveraging Kramers-Kronig relations and FFTs, the authors bypass the computational bottleneck of direct Brillouin-zone integration. The results demonstrate that the spatial decay and structure of the propagator directly encode the geometry of the Fermi surface.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, dimension-independent computational tool for studying electronic structure in doped solids, which is highly relevant for understanding transport and correlation effects in materials science.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops an efficient theoretical method to calculate real-space lattice propagators (Green's functions) for a tight-binding model when the system is doped, i.e., in the presence of a Fermi sea.

**Main result.** The method achieves a computational cost independent of the lattice dimensionality by utilizing Kramers-Kronig relations evaluated via FFTs, allowing the mapping of Fermi surface geometry onto the spatial structure of the propagators.

**Method.** The core technique involves relating the finite-$x$ propagators to the $x=0$ case using known identities and then employing Kramers-Kronig relations, which are efficiently computed for all energies simultaneously using Fast Fourier Transforms.

**Model / system.** The model is a non-interacting, tight-binding Hamiltonian defined on an arbitrary lattice, characterized by a Fermi sea determined by the carrier concentration $x$. The calculation focuses on the particle-addition part of the lattice Green's function.

**Key observables.** Real-space lattice propagators ($G_n(z; x)$), the real and imaginary parts of the propagator, and the resulting diffraction patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier concentration ($x$), Fermi energy ($E_F$), lattice distance ($n$), and energy ($\omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is valid for any lattice and dispersion, provided the $x=0$ propagators are known, and it is derived in the limit of zero broadening ($\eta 	o 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the proposed method against direct integration across 1D, 2D, and 3D lattices. Maps illustrate how the Fermi surface geometry (e.g., circular vs. square) imprints itself on the spatial structure of the propagator magnitude and sign.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, derives the necessary identities relating finite-$x$ propagators to $x=0$ counterparts, details the use of Kramers-Kronig relations via FFTs, and validates the method by comparing results across various dimensions and doping levels.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an efficient method for computing the real-space propagators (lattice Green's functions) of any tight-binding Hamiltonian in the presence of a Fermi sea with carrier concentration $x$. The method is valid for any lattice, dispersion, and dimension, provided the corresponding $x=0$ propagators are known. We show that suitable combinations of the finite-$x$ particle-addition propagators have a real or imaginary part trivially related to their $x=0$ counterpart, while the remaining part follows from a Kramers-Kronig relation that can be evaluated for all energies at once using the fast Fourier transforms. The computational cost is therefore independent of the dimensionality, unlike that of direct Brillouin-zone integration. The particle-removal propagators follow from general identities. We validate the method against direct integration for hypercubic lattices in one, two, and three dimensions, and use the 2D square lattice to illustrate how the shape of the Fermi surface is imprinted on the spatial structure of the propagators. In particular, at energies far outside the band, the propagator maps converge to the Fraunhofer diffraction pattern whose aperture is the unoccupied part of the Brillouin zone.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the ferroelectric transition in quantum ferroelectric metals using advanced optical techniques. By employing RA-SHG and pump-probe measurements, the authors confirm the order-disorder nature of the transition. Crucially, they demonstrate that femtosecond optical pulses can deterministically align the disordered polar nanoregions, opening an all-optical pathway for controlling these quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use light to deterministically write and read out polarization textures in a quantum material provides a novel, non-electrical control mechanism, which is highly relevant for understanding and manipulating quantum states in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the unsettled microscopic origin of the ferroelectric transition in quantum ferroelectric metals and seeks to develop methods to probe and control the associated polar nanoregions.

**Main result.** The study unambiguously demonstrates the order-disorder nature of the ferroelectric transition and establishes an all-optical route to deterministically control the orientation of these polar nanoregions.

**Method.** The research combines symmetry-resolved optical measurements (Rotational Anisotropy Second Harmonic Generation, RA-SHG) with ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy to track polarization dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is electron-doped strontium titanate, a prototypical quantum ferroelectric metal. The analysis models the local dipoles as tunneling between states, and the SHG response is analyzed based on fluctuations in local polarization ($\langle P^2 angle$-type).

**Key observables.** RA-SHG intensity patterns, which reveal orientational statistics; the divergence of $\Delta I_{	ext{SHG}}$ near the transition temperature ($T_{	ext{FE}}$); and the degree of alignment achieved via optical poling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), carrier concentration, and the polarization angle ($\delta$) of the incident optical pump field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the SHG signal is dominated by local dipole fluctuations and that the optical poling process can be modeled by fitting the pump-probe signal to a specific functional form.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature-dependent phase diagrams, RA-SHG patterns confirming 2-fold symmetry, and pump-probe measurements demonstrating that the optical field aligns over 80% of the polar nanoregions above the transition temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical system and the debate between ferroelectric mechanisms. It then uses RA-SHG to characterize the transition as order-disorder. Finally, it employs pump-probe spectroscopy to demonstrate deterministic, ultrafast optical control (poling) of the polar nanoregions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron-doped strontium titanate has emerged as a prototypical quantum ferroelectric metal. It provides a fertile ground to explore how ferroelectric instability intertwined with itinerant electrons creates quantum phenomena, including unconventional superconductivity. Despite extensive studies, the microscopic origin of the ferroelectric transition remains unsettled, with distinct interpretations based on displacive mechanism driven by soft mode and order-disorder alignment of local dipoles. In particular, the local dipoles form nanoscale, spatially heterogeneous clusters, termed polar nanoregions, posing a significant challenge for probing or manipulating them. Here, using rotational anisotropy second harmonic generation, a symmetry-resolved probe, we quantify the orientational statistics of polar nanoregions in dilute electron-doped Sr$_{0.95}$Ba$_{0.05}$Ti$_{1-x}$Nb$_x$O$_3$. By tracking the alignment and meltdown of polar nanoregions in thermal cycles, we unambiguously demonstrate the order-disorder nature of the ferroelectric transition. We further show that, above transition temperature, femtosecond optical fields enable deterministic control of otherwise disordered polar nanoregions, realizing reversible write and readout of polar textures on ultrafast timescales. Our findings provide new insight into ferroelectric instability and establish an all-optical route of controlling polar metal systems where conventional electrical approaches are not feasible.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes the phase-coherence length ($\lambda_{\phi}$) as the key metric for assessing dimensionality in nanoscale semiconductors. By analyzing electron-phonon coupling in Si nanosheets, the authors provide quantitative estimates for critical thicknesses, arguing that confinement is determined by how far an excitation can maintain phase coherence. This has significant implications for designing next-generation quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous focus on the phase-coherence length ($\lambda_{\phi}$) as the primary diagnostic tool provides a quantitative, advanced perspective on confinement effects, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems in solid-state devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of quantitatively determining the electronic and vibrational dimensionality of semiconductor nanostructures, such as silicon nanosheets.

**Main result.** The critical length scale for confinement is argued to be the electron and phonon phase-coherence length ($\lambda_{\phi}$), which dictates whether the system behaves as a 2DEG or approaches bulk 3D behavior.

**Method.** The analysis employs theoretical tools, including perturbation theory to calculate the electron self-energy ($\Sigma_0$) due to electron-phonon interactions, and deriving 2D phonon density of states.

**Model / system.** The primary system studied is silicon nanosheets, modeled as quantum wells or thin films, with the physics governed by electron-phonon coupling and phonon scattering processes.

**Key observables.** Electron spectral density $A(\mathbf{K}, \omega)$, electron self-energy $\Sigma_0(\mathbf{K}, \omega)$, and the effective phase-coherence length ($\lambda_{\phi}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical confinement lengths are estimated to be around 8 nm for electrons at room temperature, while phonon confinement depends heavily on wavelength (from 10 nm to $1 \mu$m).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbation theory for calculating scattering rates and assumes that the coherence length is the definitive diagnostic parameter for dimensionality.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference figures illustrating the comparison of coherence lengths to physical dimensions and the spectral broadening due to electron-phonon coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the problem of dimensionality assessment, then developing a theoretical framework using self-energy calculations for electrons, and finally extending this analysis to phonon confinement effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We discuss the problem of assessing the electronic and vibrational dimensionality of a semiconductor nanostructure: How thin and/or wide must a nanostructure be in order to induce electron and phonon confinement? Clarifying the physical justification for common criteria found in the literature, we view the electron coherence length (defined as the electron and phonon inelastic mean free path) as their `field of view' and argue (or, better yet, `speculate') that this sets the important length scale. Considering the example of Si nanosheets at room temperature, and drawing from results found in the literature, we estimate that the critical length below which electrons are subject to quantum confinement is of the order of (or smaller than) 8 nm, when their coherence length is determined by energy losses to phonons and remote phonons in gated structures. On the contrary, no single length-scale can be given for phonons: Taking their coherence length as determined by scattering with electrons and anharmonic three-phonon processes, short wavelength acoustic and optical phonons may be confined only by structures as small as 10 nm. Long-wavelength acoustic phonons, instead, may exhibit a coherence length of the order of 1 micrometer, so that they may be confined over much larger distances.

</details>

**Summary.** This work details the advantages of using ultra-low energy electrons (20–300 eV) for imaging and diffraction, highlighting their high sensitivity and low radiation damage compared to conventional TEM. It presents theoretical and experimental methods, such as Low-Energy Electron Holography, for mapping both the amplitude and phase information of biological and material structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong phase shift and the need for phase retrieval in LEEH provide a rich problem space connecting quantum wave mechanics to advanced experimental diagnostics, relevant to open quantum systems characterization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the unique physical properties and applications of ultra-low energy electrons (20–300 eV) for high-sensitivity imaging and diffraction, contrasting them with conventional high-energy electron microscopy.

**Main result.** Low-energy electrons are superior for probing surfaces and biological samples due to low radiation damage, high sensitivity to local potentials, and the ability to perform phase-sensitive measurements via holography.

**Method.** The discussion covers theoretical calculations of scattering amplitudes and differential cross-sections, alongside experimental techniques like Low-Energy Electron Diffraction (LEED) and Low-Energy Electron Holography (LEEH).

**Model / system.** The physical system involves ultra-low energy electrons interacting with matter, such as graphene, DNA, and proteins. The analysis utilizes quantum mechanical principles to model electron scattering and wave propagation.

**Key observables.** Contrast in amplitude and phase distributions, cross-correlation coefficient for radiation damage, and the complex-valued exit wave.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron energy range (20–300 eV), wavelength ($\lambda \approx 1$ Å at 150 eV), and the Inelastic Mean Free Path (IMFP) which is minimized around 100 eV.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstruction of object properties from holograms and diffraction patterns is complicated by the strong phase shift acquired by low-energy electrons.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Point Spread Functions (PSF) and Differential Cross-Sections (DCS) for different energies, show simulated complex exit waves through proteins, and demonstrate non-destructive imaging of DNA over extended periods.

**Paper structure.** The lecture progresses by detailing the unique properties of low-energy electrons (wavelength, scattering, damage), presenting the theoretical framework (calculations), and then discussing the application in specific techniques like LEED and LEEH.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The lecture discusses the properties of low-energy electrons compared to the electrons of conventional energies employed in commercial transmission electron microscopes. Low-energy electron diffraction and low-energy electron holography principles and experimental schemes are presented and discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This perspective reviews cutting-edge electron microscopy techniques designed to image ultrafast dynamics in quantum materials. By combining femtosecond time resolution with momentum-selective dark-field imaging, researchers can now microscopically track coupled electronic, lattice, and spin dynamics. This advancement opens new avenues for understanding non-equilibrium phenomena in nanoscale quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques discussed—especially momentum-resolved, time-dependent imaging—are crucial for mapping out the complex energy landscapes and phase transitions in strongly correlated electron systems, which is a major area of theoretical interest.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing techniques to simultaneously achieve high spatial resolution, ultrafast temporal resolution, and spectroscopic specificity (energy and momentum) to probe non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum materials.

**Main result.** The review highlights that combining ultrafast dark-field electron microscopy (like femtosecond photoelectron momentum microscopy and UTEM) allows for momentum-selective nano-imaging of femtosecond dynamics, providing comprehensive insights into coupled electronic and structural subsystems.

**Method.** The paper reviews advanced dark-field electron and photoelectron microscopy techniques, specifically femtosecond photoelectron momentum microscopy and ultrafast transmission electron microscopy.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on technologically relevant quantum materials, nanostructures, and devices, including 2D heterostructures (e.g., WSe2/MoS2) and charge-density wave (CDW) systems (e.g., 1T-TaS2).

**Key observables.** Electronic band structure, quasiparticle dispersion relations, structural order parameters (e.g., CDW amplitude), and ultrafast carrier dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Femtosecond timescales, nanometer spatial resolution, and momentum-resolved observables (k-space).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation addressed is that traditional techniques often average over macroscopic areas or lack momentum resolution, necessitating the development of dark-field filtering approaches.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the need for simultaneous nanometer spatial, ultrafast temporal, and spectroscopic sensitivity. Figure 2 details the concepts of dark-field filtering for momentum selection in both photoelectron microscopy and UTEM.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a review, first establishing the scientific need (Sec. I), then reviewing the general concepts of dark-field electron microscopy (Sec. II), detailing specific implementations in momentum microscopy (Sec. III), and finally presenting applications and outlook in UTEM (Sec. IV).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopies in energy and momentum have become indispensable for probing non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum materials, revealing pathways in ultrafast energy conversion and light-induced phase transitions. Different modalities uniquely access the coupled lattice, charge, and spin degrees of freedom that underpin the functionality of these materials. However, investigating technologically relevant nanoscale devices also requires high spatial resolution. Although electron microscopy provides nanometer-scale imaging, it frequently lacks the simultaneous ultrafast temporal resolution and spectroscopic specificity needed for such studies. In this perspective, we review recent advances in dark-field electron and photoelectron microscopy, focusing on two complementary techniques: femtosecond photoelectron momentum microscopy and ultrafast transmission electron microscopy. Their application enables comprehensive insights into the ultrafast dynamics of the electron, lattice, and spin subsystems. These order-selective electron imaging and spectroscopy techniques open broad scientific opportunities for a microscopic understanding of non-equilibrium phenomena in quantum materials, nanostructures and devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the fabrication and characterization of bilayer graphene mesoscopic devices, achieving record quantum mobility by electrostatically isolating the active channel. Furthermore, the study uses KPFM to map surface potentials, demonstrating that substrate plasma treatments critically control the confinement and persistence of injected charge at the graphene/oxide interface.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of high-precision quantum transport measurements with nanoscale electrostatic characterization (KPFM) provides a comprehensive view of how device fabrication and substrate chemistry dictate fundamental quantum electronic properties in 2D materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to isolate and characterize the electronic properties of bilayer graphene (BLG) in mesoscopic devices by eliminating conduction paths through natural flake edges.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated that electrostatic definition isolates the active region, yielding high quantum mobility ($\sim 2.5 	imes 10^6 	ext{ cm}^2/	ext{Vs}$) in FET geometries, while also characterizing how substrate treatments affect charge confinement using KPFM.

**Method.** The work combines advanced magnetotransport measurements (Hall-bar and FET) to probe quantum transport with Kelvin Probe Force Microscopy (KPFM) to map local electrostatic potentials after various plasma treatments.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bilayer graphene fabricated on various oxide substrates ($	ext{SiO}_2$, plasma-treated $	ext{h-BN}$). Measurements probe mesoscopic transport, Landau quantization, and the electrostatic potential landscape.

**Key observables.** Quantum mobility ($\mu_q$), quantum lifetime ($	au_q$), Landau level quantization, and Contact Potential Difference ($\Delta V_{	ext{CPD}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($B$), carrier density ($n$), and plasma treatment type ($	ext{N}_2+	ext{O}_2$ vs. $	ext{CHF}_3/	ext{O}_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the measured resistance in the gated region is equivalent to the longitudinal resistance in a Corbino geometry for some analyses, and that KPFM measurements can distinguish between intrinsic material potential and trapped surface charges.

**Figures summary.** Figures show magnetotransport data (Landau fans, $R_{xx}/R_{xy}$ traces) for both FET and Hall-bar geometries, alongside KPFM maps illustrating charge confinement differences on graphene after various plasma treatments.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating clean quantum transport in the Hall-bar/FET geometry (magnetotransport) to analyzing edge scattering effects, and finally uses KPFM to investigate the fundamental electrostatic control over the graphene/h-BN interfaces.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a graphite-gated architecture for bilayer graphene devices in which the active device is completely isolated from the natural graphene edges. Using a single patterned graphite-gate layer, we realize a fully electrostatically defined Hall-bar. Longitudinal and Hall measurements reveal mesoscopic transport features, including Hall-effect quenching and magnetoresistance peaks associated with boundary scattering. The dependence of the mesoscopic features on the carrierdensity shows that the effective channel width increases with the Fermi level and the electrostatic confinement at the gate-defined boundaries, and indicates that the carriers scatter at the electrostatic boundary. Raman spectroscopy and Kelvin probe force microscopy suggest that this boundary is disordered due to the used fabrication methods. Comparably, the quantum mobility in a fieldeffect transistor fabricated with the same architecture is not limited by boundary scattering and the visibility of quantum oscillations down to 4 mT suggests a record value of 2.5 x 10^6 cm2/Vs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates Spin-Orbit Torque (SOT) in rare-earth synthetic ferrimagnets using a combination of Sagnac magneto-optics and harmonic Hall measurements. By comparing these techniques, the authors provide a robust, quantitative determination of the SOT effective field. The findings establish these rare-earth systems as excellent platforms for designing low-power spintronic devices through precise interface engineering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the precise measurement of torque and magnetization dynamics using optical methods (MOKE) and transport measurements provides fundamental insights into interfacial coupling and spin-charge conversion, which are relevant to open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to accurately quantify the Spin-Orbit Torque (SOT) in magnetic thin films, which is crucial for developing energy-efficient spintronic devices.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that rare-earth-based synthetic ferrimagnets are a versatile platform for engineering SOT phenomena, achieving current-induced switching at low current densities via interface engineering.

**Method.** The research employs complementary techniques, combining Sagnac magneto-optical interferometry with harmonic Hall measurements to independently and quantitatively determine the damping-like SOT effective field.

**Model / system.** The physical system under investigation consists of Co/Gd-based synthetic ferrimagnets (SFiMs) and related heterostructures (e.g., Gd/Pt/Co/Al). The measurements probe the magnetization dynamics and torque generation at rare-earth/heavy-metal interfaces.

**Key observables.** Damping-like SOT effective field ($\xi_{DL}$), current-induced magnetization tilting (measured optically), and various Hall/Kerr resistances ($R_{2\omega}^{xy}$, $	heta_K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Current densities ($\ge 8-10 	ext{ MA/cm}^2$), Gd layer thickness, and multilayer stacking order.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The harmonic Hall analysis requires careful correction for nonlinear magnon contributions, and initial analyses sometimes incorrectly assumed $	ext{M}_{	ext{eff}} \gg 	ext{H}_{\parallel}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate AHE vs. field measurements, magnetotransport data showing angular dependence, and Kerr rotation vs. current/field, demonstrating the dependence of anisotropy on device geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the need for accurate SOT quantification, compares Sagnac MOKE results against harmonic Hall analysis, details the influence of interface engineering (Gd layer) on torque generation, and concludes by showing successful SOT switching in engineered structures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-orbit torque (SOT) provides an efficient route for manipulating magnetization in spintronic devices, and its accurate quantification is essential. Here, we investigate Co/Gd-based synthetic ferrimagnets using complementary magnetotransport and magneto-optical techniques. We use a Sagnac magneto-optical interferometry method to directly quantify current-induced magnetization tilting and extract the damping-like SOT effective field. The Sagnac measurements show good agreement with harmonic Hall analysis across different Co/Gd and Gd/Pt/Co heterostructures, providing a quantitative determination of the damping-like SOT effective field that does not rely on electrical transport signatures. By varying the Gd thickness and multilayer stacking order, we demonstrate that the damping-like torque is strongly influenced by spin transport and angular momentum conversion at rare-earth and heavy-metal interfaces. The observation of a finite torque in Gd/Pt/Co/Pt structures, where conventional Pt spin Hall contributions are expected to compensate, highlights the active role of the Gd layer in SOT generation. Furthermore, interface engineering enables perpendicular magnetic anisotropy in Gd/Pt/Co/Al heterostructures with a 2-nm-thick Co layer, allowing current-induced SOT switching at current densities of 8-10 MA/cm$^2$. These results establish rare-earth-based synthetic ferrimagnets as a versatile platform for engineering spin-orbit phenomena and optimizing low-power spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel, compact evaporator source integrated directly into a millikelvin STM head to overcome geometric limitations in cryogenic deposition. This setup allows for the controlled, *in-operando* deposition of isolated atoms, such as Fe, onto surfaces while maintaining high spatial resolution. This advancement is critical for performing atomic-scale experiments in extreme cryogenic environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly advances the experimental capability of manipulating quantum systems (adatoms) at ultra-low temperatures by solving a major engineering bottleneck (deposition access) in cryogenic STM setups, which is crucial for studying surface quantum phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Depositing dilute atomic ensembles onto cold samples in low-temperature scanning tunneling microscopes (STM) is challenging due to restricted internal geometries and radiation shields.

**Main result.** The authors successfully integrated a compact, low-power miniature evaporator directly into a millikelvin STM head, enabling repeated, *in-operando* deposition of isolated Fe atoms while maintaining atomic-scale surface access.

**Method.** They used a miniature incandescent lamp source coated with Fe film to deposit atoms onto MgO/Ag(100) substrates while operating the STM near 5 K, characterizing the deposition using STM imaging and IETS spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a miniature evaporator source integrated into a millikelvin STM head. Deposition occurs on crystalline substrates like MgO/Ag(100) at cryogenic temperatures, allowing for atomic-scale manipulation.

**Key observables.** Symmetric inelastic steps near $\pm 14 	ext{ mV}$ in dI/dV spectra (identifying Fe), local deposition flux ($\approx 1.5 	imes 10^{-5} 	ext{ nm}^{-2} 	ext{ s}^{-1}$), and preserved atomic-scale surface access ($< 5 	ext{ nm}$ lateral displacement).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature near 5 K, low thermal load ($\Delta T \approx 2 	ext{ K}$), and a nominal evaporator lifetime of $\sim 150 	ext{ h}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The lifetime estimate assumes a constant emission rate and unit sticking coefficient; the fixed evaporator design overcomes geometric constraints of conventional external sources.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the miniature lamp source; Figure 2 tracks the Fe film morphology; Figure 3 illustrates the 3D cutaway model of the integrated STM head.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the challenge of cryogenic deposition access, presents the novel integrated evaporator design, details the experimental procedure (deposition and characterization), and concludes by demonstrating the feasibility of repeated, low-flux deposition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Depositing dilute atomic ensembles onto cold samples is challenging in low-temperature scanning tunneling microscopes (STM) because radiation shields and restricted internal geometries often preclude a direct deposition path, particularly in instruments designed for millikelvin operation. We present a compact, milliwatt-range evaporation source fabricated from a commercial miniature incandescent lamp and integrated directly into a millikelvin STM head. The exposed tungsten filament is coated with a micrometre-thick Fe film and positioned about 1 cm from the sample. We deposit isolated Fe atoms onto MgO/Ag(100) while operating the microscope near 5 K. Evaporation increases the STM-body temperature by only about 2 K, and the same nanoscopic surface region can be readily scanned after deposition with a lateral displacement of less than 5 nm. Differential-conductance spectra displaying symmetric inelastic steps near $\pm$14 mV identify the deposited atoms on MgO as Fe. From STM images, we estimate a local deposition flux of $1.5\times10^{-5}~\mathrm{nm^{-2}\,s^{-1}}$, corresponding to a nominal evaporator lifetime of ~150 h. The fixed evaporator enables repeated low-flux deposition without a room-temperature line of sight, the need for movable radiation shields, or mechanical evaporator access after cooldown, while preserving access to the same atomic-scale surface region before and after deposition.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses a combination of STM and ARPES on Mn-doped graphene to characterize disorder. By analyzing the spectral broadening of the Dirac cone, the authors distinguish the signature of strong resonant scattering from other types of disorder. The findings establish a quantitative method for measuring defect scattering strength in graphene, which is vital for controlling electronic properties in nanoscale devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The quantitative link between a structurally identified defect (STM) and the resulting spectral broadening (ARPES) provides a powerful, measurable diagnostic tool for understanding disorder in 2D electron systems, which is crucial for designing quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to experimentally and theoretically test the spectral fingerprints associated with resonant defect scattering in graphene caused by substitutional Manganese (Mn) impurities.

**Main result.** The observed spectral broadening ($\Delta E_{	ext{DP}}$ and $\Delta k_L$) scales linearly with Mn concentration, providing strong evidence that Mn acts as a strong resonant scatterer in graphene.

**Method.** The study combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements with Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) to characterize the defect concentration and spectral signatures.

**Model / system.** The system is graphene/Cu(111) doped with substitutional Mn point defects. Theoretical modeling uses DFT to predict local electronic structure, while ARPES measures the quasiparticle spectral function $A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$.

**Key observables.** Dirac point stretching ($\Delta E_{	ext{DP}}$), momentum broadening ($\Delta k_L$), and the effective scattering cross section ($\sigma_{eff}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mn concentration ($n_{	ext{Mn}}$), temperature (20 K, 78 K), and photon energy ($\hbar
u = 34 	ext{ eV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The linear increase of $\Delta k_L$ with $n_{	ext{Mn}}$ is assumed to be dominated by single-particle scattering from the substitutional defects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM images confirming Mn placement, DFT calculations predicting Mn-related spectral weight, and ARPES spectra comparing nonimplanted, low-concentration, and high-concentration samples, illustrating the progressive spectral changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework distinguishing resonant vs. non-resonant scattering, then using STM to quantify the defect concentration, and finally applying ARPES to measure the correlated spectral changes ($\Delta E_{	ext{DP}}$ and $\Delta k_L$) that confirm the resonant scattering mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using substitutional Mn in graphene/Cu(111) as a model point defect, we combine scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) to test the predicted fingerprints of resonant scattering. As the Mn concentration increases to 0.44%, the Dirac point stretching reaches 0.52 eV, while momentum broadening remains energy independent. These spectral fingerprints classify substitutional Mn as a strong resonant scatterer and establish the combination of STM and ARPES as a powerful approach to identify and characterize resonant disorder in graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports a breakthrough in superconducting electronics by achieving a large, programmable superconducting diode effect (SDE) without an external magnetic field. The effect is realized in a $	ext{NbSe}_2/	ext{CrBr}_3$ heterostructure, where the nonreciprocity stems from a unique cubic momentum term coupled to the layered ferrimagnetism of $	ext{CrBr}_3$. This establishes a versatile platform for developing high-efficiency, memory-controlled superconducting devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it demonstrates a mechanism for controlling non-equilibrium superconducting currents using magnetic memory effects in 2D heterostructures, which is a key area for advanced quantum electronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is achieving a large, controllable superconducting diode effect (SDE) at zero external magnetic field, as most existing realizations require an external field.

**Main result.** The authors report a field-free SDE with a high efficiency (35.7%) in a vdW $	ext{NbSe}_2/	ext{CrBr}_3$ heterostructure, demonstrating that the diode polarity is programmable by the magnetic field history.

**Method.** The study combines transport measurements, micromagnetic simulations, and a generalized Ginzburg-Landau theory to analyze the nonreciprocal supercurrent.

**Model / system.** The system is a van der Waals heterostructure composed of the superconductor $	ext{NbSe}_2$ interfaced with the layered magnetic insulator $	ext{CrBr}_3$. The nonreciprocity is theoretically linked to a symmetry-allowed cubic term odd in the Cooper-pair momentum.

**Key observables.** Superconducting diode efficiency ($\eta$), nonreciprocal critical currents ($	ext{I}_{c+}$ vs $	ext{I}_{c-}$), and the hysteretic/nonmonotonic reversal of the diode polarity upon magnetic field sweeping.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The efficiency reached (35.7%), the specific material combination ($	ext{NbSe}_2/	ext{CrBr}_3$), and the dependence on the magnetic field history.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The SDE mechanism is attributed to the layered ferrimagnetism in $	ext{CrBr}_3$ creating a history-dependent interfacial exchange field, distinguishing it from conventional mechanisms like Meissner screening.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the field-free SDE confirmation via differential resistance curves, map the efficiency ($\eta$) dependence on magnetic field showing hysteresis, and detail the theoretical link between the proximity field and the observed polarity reversals.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (field-free SDE), presents the physical platform ($	ext{NbSe}_2/	ext{CrBr}_3$), develops the theoretical mechanism (cubic momentum term driven by layered ferrimagnetism), and validates the findings through comprehensive transport measurements and simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The superconducting diode effect (SDE), characterized by nonreciprocal dissipationless supercurrent, offers a promising route toward ultralow-power superconducting electronics. Yet most realizations require an external magnetic field, and achieving a large, controllable SDE at zero field remains challenging. Here we report a field-free SDE with an efficiency reaching 35.7% in a van der Waals NbSe$_2$/CrBr$_3$ heterostructure, whose polarity is programmable by magnetic-field history. Unlike conventional mechanisms based on finite-momentum pairing, the SDE originates from the leading symmetry-allowed cubic term odd in Cooper-pair momentum, which yields unequal critical currents while leaving the equilibrium condensate at zero momentum. Remarkably, upon sweeping a perpendicular magnetic field, the diode polarity undergoes nonmonotonic and hysteretic reversals that cannot be explained by Meissner screening or conventional ferromagnetic proximity. Combining transport measurements, micromagnetic simulations, and a generalized Ginzbur-Landau theory, we attribute these unconventional behaviors to layered ferrimagnetism in CrBr$_3$, where coexisting ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic interlayer couplings produce a history-dependent interfacial exchange field acting on NbSe$_2$. Our results reveal a distinct mechanism for nonreciprocal superconductivity and establish layered van der Waals magnetism as a versatile platform for high-efficiency, programmable, field-free superconducting diodes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the successful synthesis of high-$T_c$ superconducting $	ext{LaH}_{10\pm\delta}$ films using a novel film precursor approach in Diamond Anvil Cells. The researchers confirmed superconductivity with $T_c$ near 250 K and, critically, demonstrated that both the crystal structure and the superconducting state remain stable for hundreds of days post-synthesis. This establishes a practical route for creating and studying high-pressure superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the study of high-$T_c$ superconductivity in novel materials under extreme conditions touches upon strongly correlated electron physics, which is a major area of interest for theoretical condensed matter physicists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to synthesize, characterize, and assess the long-term stability of high-temperature superconducting hydrides, specifically LaH10, under extreme high-pressure conditions.

**Main result.** The authors successfully synthesized LaH10 films, confirming a high critical temperature ($T_c \approx 247 	ext{ K}$) and demonstrating remarkable structural and superconducting stability over several hundred days post-synthesis.

**Method.** The research combines in situ laser heating synthesis within Diamond Anvil Cells (DACs) with structural characterization using synchrotron X-ray diffraction (XRD) and electrical transport measurements to probe superconductivity.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the high-pressure hydride superconductor LaH10, which is synthesized as thin films on elemental lanthanum precursors within a DAC. Measurements track phase coexistence (e.g., LaH10, LaH9, LaH4) and superconducting properties under sustained high pressure.

**Key observables.** Critical temperature ($T_c$), upper critical magnetic field ($H_{c2}$), superconducting transition width ($\Delta T_c(B)$), crystal structure (fcc $	ext{La}$ sublattice, $	ext{Fm}ar{3}	ext{m}$), and temporal stability over $\sim 300$ days.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High pressures (168-176 GPa), $T_c \approx 250 	ext{ K}$, and long time scales ($\sim 300$ days).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The unit cell volume derived from XRD reflects the fcc $	ext{La}$ sublattice, which may not fully capture the stoichiometry of the entire hydride phase.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail XRD patterns showing phase distribution, spatial X-ray mapping of different hydride phases, and time-dependent measurements of $T_c$ and structural parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure detailing the synthesis method (PVD precursors in DACs), structural confirmation via XRD, measurement of superconducting properties ($T_c$, $H_{c2}$), and finally, a long-term stability assessment over months.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-pressure hydrides hold the record for the highest superconducting critical temperatures across all classes of superconductors. Currently lanthanum decahydride, LaH$_{10}$, exhibits the highest critical temperature among binaries, with $T_c \approx$ 250 K at pressures between 140-180 GPa. Here, we report the synthesis of LaH$_{10\pmδ}$ films in two DACs at pressures of 168 GPa and 176 GPa via in situ laser heating of elemental lanthanum films with ammonia borane (NH$_3$BH$_3$) as the hydrogen donor. The high-symmetry fcc lanthanum sublattice (space group $Fm\bar3m$) is resolved using synchrotron X-ray diffraction, with unit cell parameters in excellent agreement with previous studies on bulk samples. We provide confirmation of high-$T_c$ superconductivity in LaH$_{10\pmδ}$ with highest $T_c$ of 247 K at 176 GPa evidenced in electrical measurements. The characteristic suppression of superconductivity is observed in magnetic fields. Furthermore, combined diffraction and electrical measurements reveal remarkable temporal stability of both the crystal structure and the high-$T_c$ superconductivity over the full measurement period of about 300 days post laser heating. Our work establishes film precursors using physical vapour deposition (PVD) techniques as a practical route to hydride formation, opening a pathway toward the controlled synthesis of promising ternary hydrides and the integration of micro-fabricated device geometries in diamond anvil cells.

</details>
