**Summary.** This theoretical study demonstrates that in rhombohedral graphene, specific layer-dependent pairing interactions can stabilize a finite-momentum Kekulé superconducting state. By analyzing the competition between different pairing channels using mean-field theory, the authors show that this state can emerge while maintaining crucial symmetries like time-reversal and inversion symmetry. This work highlights the role of orbital structure and band chirality in engineering unconventional superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, symmetry-preserving mechanism for unconventional superconductivity in a tunable 2D material, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum electronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the mechanism for realizing finite-momentum superconductivity (Pair-Density-Wave, PDW) in a manner that preserves both time-reversal and inversion symmetries.

**Main result.** Orbital-dependent interactions in the presence of band chirality favor the formation of a Kekulé superconducting state with finite center-of-mass momentum, even in time-reversal and inversion symmetric Dirac materials.

**Method.** The study employs mean-field theory applied to the pairing problem, comparing the superconducting instabilities in the zero-momentum ($Q=0$) and finite-momentum ($Q=\pm 2K_D$) sectors.

**Model / system.** The system is rhombohedral multilayer graphene (RMG) described by an extended chiral Kane-Mele Hamiltonian. The analysis focuses on how layer/orbital-dependent pairing interactions drive the superconducting instability.

**Key observables.** Superconducting order parameters ($\Delta_{Q=0}$ vs $\Delta_{Q=\pm 2KD}$), superfluid stiffness, and the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition temperature ($T_{BKT}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer-dependent interaction signs ($V_{1A} = -V_{JB}$), the Kane-Mele mass term ($\lambda$), and the critical carrier density ($n_{crit}^K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily uses onsite interactions and initially neglects remote-hopping effects, though later notes suggest layer-dependent renormalization effects can relax this assumption.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference schematics illustrating the competition between $Q=0$ (inter-valley) and $Q=\pm 2KD$ (intra-valley) Cooper-pair scattering processes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using the Kane-Mele model, then analyzing the pairing instabilities in different momentum sectors using mean-field theory, and finally mapping the phase diagram to show the conditions favoring the finite-momentum Kekulé state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

At weak coupling, finite-momentum superconductivity is typically associated with broken time-reversal or inversion symmetry of the Fermi surface. Here, we show that lattice-scale pair-density-wave order in rhombohedral multilayer graphene can arise from layer/orbital-dependent pairing interactions, band chirality, and Dirac-point-centered Fermi surface topology while preserving both symmetries. Using mean-field theory and comparing finite momentum sectors $Q = \pm 2 K_{D}$ with the $ Q = 0$ superconducting state, we find that layer-dependent interactions of opposite signs ($V_{1A}=-V_{JB}=-|V|$) favor an intra-valley Kekulè state with center-of-mass momentum ($ Q=\pm 2 K_D$). In the presence of a time-reversal and inversion symmetry-preserving Kane-Mele mass ($λ$), this state appears only above a critical carrier density ($n^{crit}_{K}(λ,J)$). The two superconducting condensates exhibit opposite chirality, $J(-J)$ for $K_D(-K_D) $ valleys, thereby preserving time-reversal and inversion symmetry. We map the phase diagram and analyze the dependence of $T_c$ on the chirality index $J$ and $λ$. We also evaluate the superfluid stiffness in the Kekulè superconducting state, thereby determining the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition temperature. Our results show that orbital-dependent interactions in the presence of band chirality favor finite-momentum pairing in time-reversal and inversion symmetric Dirac materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous mathematical analysis of the long-time asymptotics for the defocusing Wadati-Konno-Ichikawa (WKI) equation on a complex, finite-genus background. It utilizes the powerful machinery of the Riemann-Hilbert problem and nonlinear steepest descent analysis. The result characterizes the solution's behavior across different spatial and temporal regimes, providing deep insight into the system's integrable dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of advanced integrable system techniques (RH method, algebro-geometry) to analyze the long-time dynamics of a nonlinear wave equation is highly relevant to theoretical physics, particularly in nonlinear optics and quantum field theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the long-time asymptotic behavior of the integrable defocusing Wadati-Konno-Ichikawa (WKI) equation when posed on a finite-genus algebro-geometric background.

**Main result.** The long-time asymptotics are derived for the defocusing WKI equation in four distinct space-time regions (Transition I, II, ZM, Fast-decay), showing the solution is a sum of the background solution and specific correction terms.

**Method.** The analysis heavily employs the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method applied to the Riemann-Hilbert (RH) problem formulation of the finite-genus Baker-Akhiezer function.

**Model / system.** The system is governed by the Wadati-Konno-Ichikawa (WKI) equation, an integrable nonlinear evolution equation with a saturable nonlinearity. The background is defined by a finite-genus algebro-geometric structure.

**Key observables.** The WKI field $q(x, t)$ and the reciprocal coordinate $y(x, t)$ are the primary observables analyzed for their asymptotic behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis distinguishes between the defocusing case ($\sigma = -1$) and involves parameters related to the spectral plane and the structure of the Riemann surface.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the standard small-norm Riemann-Hilbert theory for the auxiliary function $E(z)$ for sufficiently large positive time $t$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 15 illustrates the contours $\Sigma(E)$ used in the Riemann-Hilbert problem setup for the analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the finite-genus Baker-Akhiezer function via solvable matrix RH problems, yielding theta-function representations. It then applies the Deift-Zhou steepest descent analysis to the defocusing WKI Cauchy problem, dividing the space-time plane into four regions (I, II, III, IV) to derive the asymptotic solution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the finite-genus algebro-geometric solutions of the Wadati-Konno-Ichikawa (WKI) equation with the saturable nonlinearity and long-time asymptotic behaviors of their short-range perturbations. First, for both the focusing and defocusing reductions, we formulate the finite-genus Baker-Akhiezer functions as explicitly solvable the matrix Riemann-Hilbert (RH) problems on the complex spectral plane and obtain theta-function representations together with the reconstruction formulae for the WKI field and the reciprocal coordinate. We then consider the Cauchy problem of the defocusing WKI equation on a finite-genus algebro-geometric background. We construct the scattering data and RH problem, and perform a Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent analysis. The space-time plane is divided into two transition regions, a Zakharov-Manakov (ZM) region, and a fast-decay region. The leading term is a phase-shifted finite-genus WKI solution. The transition corrections are governed by a Painlevé-XXXIV model, while the ZM radiation is described by parabolic-cylinder functions. The reciprocal-coordinate asymptotics are obtained simultaneously.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the interplay between antiferromagnetism and superconductivity in lightly-doped single-layer cuprates. By measuring magnetization and resistivity, the authors argue that superconductivity is intrinsically linked to the robust, strongly correlated antiferromagnetic background of the $\mathrm{Cu-O}$ planes. The findings highlight the coexistence of these two orders in the superconducting state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong correlation between magnetism (AF order) and superconductivity in cuprates is a cornerstone problem in condensed matter physics, making this experimental work highly relevant for understanding unconventional pairing mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To uncover the intrinsic magnetism and electrical conduction mechanisms in lightly-doped single-layer cuprates that exhibit both antiferromagnetic (AF) and superconducting (SC) orders simultaneously.

**Main result.** The high-temperature superconductivity is attributed to conduction holes hosted within a robust, strongly correlated AF ordered state of localized Cu 3d electrons.

**Method.** The study employs multiple experimental techniques, including magnetization measurements, resistivity measurements ($ho_{\parallel}, ho_{\perp}$), and NQR spectroscopy, across varying doping levels.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the lightly-doped single-layer cuprate $\mathrm{La}_2\mathrm{CuO}_{4+\delta}$ (LCO), which is studied by comparing superconducting samples ($p \approx 0.03$) to more insulating/AF samples ($p \lesssim 0.015$).

**Key observables.** Magnetization ($M$), electrical resistivity ($ho$), sub-lattice moments ($M_s$), AF couplings ($J_{\parallel}$), and the development of 2D AF spin correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping level ($p \approx 0.03$ vs $p \lesssim 0.015$), Néel temperature ($T_N = 266 	ext{ K}$), and the superconducting critical temperature ($T_c \sim 30 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the superconducting state arises from the doping of a highly localized, robust antiferromagnetically ordered background.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$) and magnetization ($M$) near $T_N$, and show the correlation between field-induced weak ferromagnetism transitions and sharp reductions in resistivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares magnetic and transport properties across different doping regimes, focusing on the anomalous behavior observed below a characteristic temperature $T^*$, and linking these anomalies to the coexistence of AF and SC orders.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The temperature dependences of magnetization and electrical resistivity as well as their magnetic field dependences have been examined in lightly-doped single-layer cuprate $\mathrm{La}_2\mathrm{CuO}_{4+δ}$ (LCO, hole-doping level $p \, (2δ) \cong 0.03$) single crystals, in comparison with those in the extremely low doping region of $p \lesssim 0.015$ to uncover the intrinsic magnetism and electrical conduction of the $\mathrm{Cu\text{-}O}$ plane that exhibits both antiferromagnetic (AF) and superconducting (SC) orders simultaneously. In $p \cong 0.03$ SC LCO, the sub-lattice moments on $\mathrm{Cu}$ sites and their AF couplings are only $\sim 15\,\%$ smaller than those of the Mott-insulator parent material, suggesting that the localization of $\mathrm{Cu}$ $3d$ electrons remains very strong. Furthermore, we report that in the SC LCO, two-dimensional AF spin correlations develop rapidly from $T^* \cong 280\text{ K}$ towards Néel temperature $T_{\mathrm{N}} = 266\text{ K}$, where the out-of-plane resistivity starts to decrease largely. This might be responsible for the AF ordering at such a high temperature in the SC single-layer cuprate with $p \cong 0.03$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to investigate the thermodynamics and stability of gas nanofilms confined by liquid phases. Key findings include the exponential decrease of surface tension and a morphological transition to spherical bubbles upon thinning. The research highlights the importance of thermal fluctuations in accurately modeling these nanoscale interfacial phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides detailed molecular insights into phase transitions and interfacial thermodynamics in confined geometries, which are fundamental concepts in statistical mechanics and many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the poorly understood thermodynamics and stability of free gas nanofilms confined between bulk liquid phases.

**Main result.** The surface tension decreases exponentially with film thickness, and the film undergoes a morphological transition from planar to spherical bubble upon thinning. The discrepancy between MD and cDFT is attributed to thermal capillary-wave fluctuations.

**Method.** Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations are employed alongside a Gibbs surface thermodynamic framework to derive relationships between surface tension and disjoining pressure.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a gas nanofilm confined between two bulk liquid phases, using Lennard-Jones argon as a representative model system.

**Key observables.** Surface tension ($\sigma_f$), disjoining pressure ($\Pi$), film thickness ($h$), and gas-phase morphology.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Film thickness ($h$), temperature ($T$), and lateral surface area ($A$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Gibbs surface formulation and contrasts the system behavior with established liquid nanofilm models, noting the importance of including thermal fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of surface tension and disjoining pressure on film thickness and temperature, and show time evolution of gas-phase morphologies (planar to spherical bubble).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the thermodynamic framework, derives key relationships between surface tension and disjoining pressure, uses MD simulations to calculate these properties, and analyzes the resulting morphological transitions and theoretical discrepancies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanometer-thick fluid films play a critical role in confined multiphase processes, yet the thermodynamics and stability of free gas nanofilms remain poorly understood compared with their liquid counterparts. Here, molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are employed to systematically investigate gas nanofilms confined between bulk liquid phases using Lennard-Jones argon as a model system. The results show that the surface tension decreases exponentially with decreasing film thickness, accompanied by an increasing magnitude of the negative disjoining pressure. Upon thinning, the planar gas film undergoes a distinct morphological transition from a stable planar state to a transient or persistent spherical bubble through the formation and growth of a liquid bridge. The film surface area strongly affects its thermodynamic properties, with larger areas producing stronger thickness dependence and larger deviations from classical density functional theory (cDFT) predictions. The closer agreement between MD and cDFT at smaller surface areas suggests that the discrepancy primarily arises from thermal capillary-wave fluctuations, which are included in MD but omitted in mean-field cDFT. Moreover, at small film thicknesses, the magnitude of the disjoining pressure increases with decreasing temperature, consistent with the enhanced sensitivity of the confined gas phase to thickness variations and contrasting with the trend generally reported for liquid nanofilms. These findings provide molecular insights into the thermodynamics and stability of gas nanofilms, with implications for confined multiphase transport and droplet coalescence.

</details>
